) we conclude that the RHS of (
22
) is
o(1)
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Theorem

3.3
can cover other CV variants than the standard
K
-fold CV, such as repeated random split, where
I_{\mathrm{tr}}
is repeatedly generated by uniformly sampling
n_{\mathrm{tr}}
indices from
[n]
without replacement and
I_{\mathrm{te}}=[n]\backslash I_{\mathrm{tr}}
. Another example is “reversed
K
-fold CV”, which uses the same
K
-fold split but
I_{\mathrm{tr}}=I_{n,k}
and
I_{\mathrm{te}}=I_{n,-k}
for
k\in[K]
.

Remark 3.6
.

We briefly discuss more concrete versions of the sufficient conditions implied by
Theorem

3.3
in standard parametric and nonparametric regression models.

1.

Parametric case. When both models are parametric and correctly specified, then
\Psi_{2,n}\asymp 1/n
and
Theorem

3.3
requires
n_{\mathrm{te}}/n_{\mathrm{tr}}\rightarrow\infty
, which turns out to be tight as seen in
Example

3.4
. Such a split ratio is not achievable by any
K
-fold split, but achievable by reversed
K
-fold split with a diverging
K
.

2.

Nonparametric case. When
\Psi_{2,n}\asymp n^{-\alpha}
for
\alpha\in[0,1)
,
Theorem

3.3
implies that
n_{\mathrm{te}}=\omega(n^{\alpha})
is sufficient for model selection consistency using sample splitting and hence
K
-fold CV with finite
K
.

Remark 3.7
.

The only reason to require
K=O(1)
in
Theorem

3.3
is to allow a simple union bound so that the consistency of a single split carries over to the
K
-split case. If we have specific rates of convergence in the conditions (
17
) and (
18
), then we can allow
K
to grow slowly as allowed in the union bound argument.

3.2
Online Model Selection

In this subsection we introduce the online model selection problem, setting the stage for a variant of cross-validation tailored for the online setting.

So far we have only considered the batch setting, where the fitting algorithm
\hat{f}
takes in a static dataset
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
of an arbitrary sample size
n
and outputs
\hat{f}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\in\mathcal{F}
. The online setting, which becomes increasingly popular in large-scale machine learning, differs from the batch setting with profound algorithmic and computational consequences.

Definition 3.8

(Online Estimator)
.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be the sample space of a single data point and
\mathcal{F}
a parameter space.
An online algorithm
\hat{f}=(\hat{f}_{n}:n\geq 1)
is an infinite sequence of mappings such that

(a)

\hat{f}_{1}:{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto\mathcal{F}

(b)

For
i\geq 2
,
\hat{f}_{i}:\mathcal{F}\times{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto\mathcal{F}
.

(c)

After obtaining
\hat{f}_{i}
, the sample point
\{X_{j}:j\leq i\}
and previous estimates
\{\hat{f}_{j}:j<i\}
are not used again by the algorithm in the future.

An online estimator
\hat{f}=(\hat{f}_{i}:i\geq 1)
takes in a (possibly infinite) stream of sample points
(X_{i}:i\geq 1)
in
{\mathcal{X}}
, with an initial estimate
\hat{f}_{0}
, and updates the estimate from
\hat{f}_{i}
to
\hat{f}_{i+1}=\hat{f}_{i+1}(\hat{f}_{i},X_{i+1})
after seeing the
(i+1)
th sample point (
i\geq 0
). Part (c) ensures that the online algorithm satisfies the “single-pass” property, meaning that the sample points and estimates are only processed at the time of sampling and will not be stored. This does not exclude the popular Polyak averaging estimate that outputs the final estimate as the average of
(\hat{f}_{i}:i\geq 1)
, because we only need to store the current average estimate, which takes a constant storage space.

Online algorithms often come with great computational efficiency and play a crucial role in modern machine learning.
Perhaps the most important example of such online estimators is stochastic gradient descent.

Example 3.9

(Stochastic Gradient Descent)
.

Suppose the estimation target is
f^{*}=\arg\min\mathbb{E}_{X}\varphi(f,X)
, for an objective function
\varphi(\cdot,\cdot)
. The stochastic gradient descent estimate
\hat{f}_{n}
estimates
f^{*}
by minimizing the empirical average objective function
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi(f,X_{i})
using an online update scheme.

(i)

Initialize
\hat{f}_{0}=0

(ii)

For each
i\geq 1
,
\hat{f}_{i}=\hat{f}_{i-1}-\alpha_{i}\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{i-1},X_{i})
, where
\dot{\varphi}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the partial gradient of
\varphi(\cdot,\cdot)
with respect to the first argument.

The sequence
(\alpha_{i}:i\geq 1)
specify the learning rate at each step.

To provide a more concrete example and better connect to recent literature, we consider the following variant of “sieve-SGD” tailored for nonparametric regression under squared loss, where each sample point
X=(Y,Z)
, with
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{1}
and
\mathcal{F}
being a class of functions that map from
\mathcal{Z}
to
\mathbb{R}^{1}
. Let
(\phi_{j}:j\geq 1)
be an orthonormal basis functions in
L_{2,Z}
. The sieve-SGD initializes
\hat{f}_{0}=0
and the update from
\hat{f}_{i-1}
to
\hat{f}_{i}
is:

\hat{f}_{i}=\hat{f}_{i-1}+\alpha_{i}\left[Y_{i}-\hat{f}_{i-1}(Z_{i})\right]\sum_{j=1}^{J_{i}}j^{-w}\phi_{j}(Z_{i})\phi_{j}\,.

(24)

Here
\alpha_{i}
is the learning rate,
J_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
is the number of basis functions used at sample size
i
, and
w
is a shrinkage exponent. It has been shown in
Zhang and Simon 2022
that with suitable choices of
(\alpha_{i},J_{i})
and
w
, the sieve-SGD estimate nearly achieves the minimax optimal error rate over Sobolev classes of functions.

When model selection is of interest, a fundamental difference emerges between the batch setting and the online setting. In batch setting, the evaluation criterion for model selection is often tied to a particular sample size, which is typically
n
but could also be
n_{\mathrm{tr}}
such as in
K
-fold cross-validation. The batch cross-validation can approximate the quantities
\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}
and
\bar{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
because the algorithm can access versions of
\hat{f}({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})
through
\hat{f}({\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k})
and repeatedly evaluate their losses at many independent sample points. This is not possible in the online setting, where, as specified in
Definition

3.8
, for each sample size
i
, the loss function of the estimate
\hat{f}_{i}
at sample size
i
can only be evaluated once using
\ell(X_{i+1},\hat{f}_{i})
. As a result, the model selection criterion in the online setting must have the following two distinct features.

1.

The model selection criterion in the online setting needs to be asymptotic, as it must describe the behavior of an infinite sequence.

2.

As a consequence, in the online setting, a candidate in the model selection problem must be a sequence itself, which specifies the update rule for each sample size.

Given these thoughts, suppose we have
m
online algorithms,
(\hat{f}_{i}^{(r)}:i\geq 1)
for
r=1,...,m
, we can define the online model selection target as the
r^{*}\in[m]
such that, assuming its existence,

\max_{r\neq r^{*}}\limsup_{i\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi_{r^{*},i}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi_{r,i}\right]}<1\,,

(25)

where
\Psi_{r,i}
is the excess risk of the fitted model
\hat{f}_{i}^{(r)}
, as defined in (
16
) in the squared loss regression setting.

Such an optimal
r^{*}
naturally exists in many nonparametric regression settings. Consider the sieve-SGD example in (
24
). For simplicity suppose we only need to choose the number of basis
(J_{i}:i\geq 1)
. Existing theory
Zhang and Simon 2022
suggests the optimal choice is
J_{i}=Bi^{\tau}
for a pair of positive constants
(B,\tau)
that depends on the function class. Thus, the candidate set of
m
online algorithms correspond to
m
distinct combinations
\{(B^{(r)},\tau^{(r)}):r=1,...,m\}
, where for each
r\in[m]
, the corresponding online algorithm uses the update rule (
24
) with
J_{i}=B^{(r)}i^{\tau^{(r)}}
.

In this example, an online model selection procedure should point us to a choice of sequence
\{J_{i}^{(r)}:i\geq 1\}
that will eventually become the best for each
i
when the sample size keeps increasing. In contrast, standard batch model selection methods only focus on a single sample size and do not suggest a good choice of model for a different sample size. For example, if batch model selection returns a choice of
J=10
at
sample size
100
, it is unclear whether this
J
comes from a
(B,\tau)
combination of
(1,~1/2)
or
(\sqrt{10},~1/4)
, which would lead to different choices of
J
for other values of sample size.

3.3
Rolling Validation

In this subsection we present an online version of the LOOCV model selection method, and establish consistency results under the same linear regression setting and squared loss as in
Section

3.1
.

Recall that in the regression model we have
X_{i}=(Y_{i},Z_{i})
such that
Y_{i}=f^{*}(Z_{i})+\epsilon_{i}
with zero-mean noise
\epsilon_{i}
independent of
Z_{i}
. The loss function is
\ell(x,f)=(y-f(z))^{2}
.

Suppose we have two sequences of online estimates of
f^{*}
:
(\hat{f}_{i}^{(r)}:i\geq 1)
for
r\in\{1,2\}
satisfying
Definition

3.8
.
Again, the excess risk
\Psi_{r,n}
, defined in (
16
), will play an important role.
Unlike in the batch setting, where we used the stochastic version of model quality gap (
3.1
). In the online setting we will use a more transparent approach, where the model quality gap is stated in the expected excess risks

\psi_{r,n}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\Psi_{r,n}\,.

(26)

We will assume that the expected excess risks converge at a certain speed, which serves the purpose of model quality comparison.

Assumption 3.10

(Model quality)
.

There exists
0\leq a_{2}\leq a_{1}\leq 1
such that

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{a_{1}}\psi_{1,n}<\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{a_{2}}\psi_{2,n}\leq\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}n^{a_{2}}\psi_{2,n}<\infty\,.

3.10
involves a chain of three inequalities. The most crucial one is the first inequality, which implies that the expected excess risk of model
1
is less than that of model
2
with a sufficient gap. When the two models have the same rates of convergence (
a_{1}=a_{2}
) the strict inequality ensures that the model
1
is better in a similar flavor as
3.1
.

Although it is more convenient to describe the model quality gap in terms of the expected excess risk
\psi_{r,n}
, it would also require additional argument to guarantee concentration of
\Psi_{r,n}
around
\psi_{r,n}
. This will be achieved through stability conditions in
3.11
below.

Assumption 3.11

(Estimator stability)
.

There exists positive constants
c
,
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
such that, for
j\leq i
,
r=1,2
,

\mathbb{E}\left\{\left[\nabla_{j}\hat{f}^{(r)}_{i}(X_{i+1})\right]^{2}\bigg|{\mathcal{D}}_{j}\right\}\leq ci^{-2b_{r}}\,,~~\text{almost surely}\,.

(27)

Recall that
\nabla_{j}
is the perturb-
j
th-entry operator defined in (
5
). The almost-sure bound condition can be relaxed to
L_{q}
norm bounds with different choices of conjugate indices in Hölder’s inequality and requiring stronger moment conditions on the risk function. We provide examples satisfying (
27
) in
Section

6.4
.

We consider the following online
rolling validation
risk estimate.

\Xi_{r,n}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(X_{i+1},\hat{f}^{(r)}_{i})\,.

(28)

The RV risk estimate has some promising features.

1.

The RV risk estimate
\Xi_{r,n}
resembles the leave-one-out cross-validation, as each fitted model
\hat{f}_{i}^{(r)}
—fitted using all sample points up to time
i
—is evaluated at one independent sample point
X_{i+1}
.

2.

The RV risk estimate
\Xi_{r,n}
is fully compatible with the online learning protocol described in
Definition

3.8
, as one can obtain
\Xi_{r,n+1}
from
(\Xi_{r,n},X_{n+1},\hat{f}_{n}^{(r)})
without requiring access to any
\Xi_{r,i}
,
X_{i}
, or
\hat{f}_{i}^{(r)}
for
i<n
.

3.

When
n
becomes large,
\Xi_{r,n}
is increasingly representative of the asymptotic behavior of
\Psi_{r,n}
and
\psi_{r,n}
, which serves the purpose of online model selection objective in (
25
).

The intuition is that, if the fitting procedure does not too heavily rely on any single sample point as implied by the stability condition (
27
), then the contribution of each single sample point to
\Psi_{r,i}
will vanish as
i
increases. Thus each single sample point still only contributes a small proportion of the sum
\Xi_{r,n}
, and hence making the sum concentrate around its expected value. This is formally stated in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.12

(Model Selection Consistency of Rolling Validation)
.

Under the regression model (
15
) and squared loss function,
assume that the function space is uniformly bounded:
\|f(Z)\|_{\infty}\leq B<\infty
for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
.
If
3.10
and
3.11
hold with

\displaystyle b_{1}>

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+a_{2}-\frac{a_{1}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle b_{2}>

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+\frac{a_{2}}{2}\,,

then the rolling validation risks
\Xi_{r,n}
defined in (
28
) satisfy

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(\Xi_{1,n}<\Xi_{2,n})=1\,.

Proof of
Theorem

3.12
.

Denote
\delta_{r,n}=(\hat{f}^{(r)}_{n}-f^{*})(Z_{n+1})
,
U_{n}=\delta_{1,n}^{2}-\delta_{2,n}^{2}
. We have

\displaystyle\Xi_{1,n}-\Xi_{2,n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[U_{i}-2\epsilon_{i+1}(\delta_{1,i}-\delta_{2,i})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{-2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i+1}(\delta_{1,i}-\delta_{2,i})}_{(I)}+\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[U_{i}-\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{i})\right]}_{(II)}+\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{i})-\mathbb{E}U_{i}\right]}_{(III)}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\psi_{1,i}-\psi_{2,i})\,,

where, recall the notation
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}=(X_{j}:1\leq j\leq i)
.
The first three terms are mean zero noises and the last term is the signal, which will be negative for large values of
n
. The plan is to derive upper bounds for each of the noise terms so that their sum is unlikely to override the signal. In this proof, since the claimed result is consistency, we will not keep track of all constant factors. To simplify notation, we will use “
\lesssim
” to denote that the inequality holds up to a constant factor.

Controlling term (I):
Term (I) has zero mean and martingale increments, and can be bounded similarly as in the proof of
Theorem

3.3
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i+1}(\delta_{1,i}-\delta_{2,i})\right)=

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\delta_{1,i}-\delta_{2,i})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sigma^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\psi_{1,i}+\psi_{2,i})

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}i^{-a_{1}}+i^{-a_{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle n^{1-a_{2}}\vee\log n\,,

since by assumption
a_{2}\leq a_{1}
.

Controlling term (II):
Term (II) also has zero mean and martingale increments.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[U_{i}-\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{i})\right]\right)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[U_{i}-\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{i})\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}U_{i}^{2}\leq 2B^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\psi_{1,i}+\psi_{2,i})

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle n^{1-a_{2}}\vee\log n\,.

Controlling term (III):
Term (III) does not have martingale increments. Each term
\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{i})-\mathbb{E}U_{i}
is a function of
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
, which involves all sample points
(X_{j}:1\leq j\leq i)
.
We will use a further martingale decomposition of this term.

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{i})-\mathbb{E}U_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{i}\left\{\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{j})-\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{j-1})\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{i=j}^{n}\left[\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{j})-\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{j-1})\right]

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}M_{n,j}

where
M_{n,j}=\sum_{i=j}^{n}\left[\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{j})-\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{j-1})\right]
, is a martingale increment sequence with respect to the filtration
({\mathcal{D}}_{j}:j\geq 1)
. Now we control the
L_{2}
norm of
M_{n,j}
. Observe that

\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{j})-\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{j-1})=\mathbb{E}(\nabla_{j}U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{j})\,,

where the
\nabla
notation is defined in (
5
).

Recall that
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}^{j}=(X_{1},...,X_{j}^{\prime},...,X_{n})
is the perturb-one dataset obtained by replacing the
j
th entry of
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
by an i.i.d. copy
X_{j}^{\prime}
. Let
\delta_{r,i}^{j}
be the counterpart of
\delta_{r,i}
obtained as if the input dataset were
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}^{j}
.
By construction,

\nabla_{j}U_{i}=(\nabla_{j}\delta_{1,i})(\delta_{1,i}+\delta_{1,i}^{j})-(\nabla_{j}\delta_{2,i})(\delta_{2,i}+\delta_{2,i}^{j}).

We have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbb{E}(\nabla_{j}U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{j})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[(\nabla_{j}\delta_{1,i})\delta_{1,i}\big|{\mathcal{D}}_{j}\right]\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[(\nabla_{j}\delta_{1,i})\delta_{1,i}^{j}\Big|{\mathcal{D}}_{j}\right]\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[(\nabla_{j}\delta_{2,i})\delta_{2,i}\big|{\mathcal{D}}_{j}\right]\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[(\nabla_{j}\delta_{2,i})\delta_{2,i}^{j}\Big|{\mathcal{D}}_{j}\right]\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle i^{-b_{1}}\psi_{1,i}^{1/2}+i^{-b_{2}}\psi_{2,i}^{1/2}

where the last step follows from conditional Hölder’s inequality and conditional Jensen’s inequality.
By
3.10
,
\psi_{r,i}\lesssim i^{-a_{r}}
, and hence

\displaystyle\|M_{n,j}\|_{2}=

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=j}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\nabla_{j}U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{j})\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{i=j}^{n}i^{-b_{1}}\psi_{1,i}^{1/2}+i^{-b_{2}}\psi_{2,i}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{i=j}^{n}i^{-b_{1}-a_{1}/2}+i^{-b_{2}-a_{2}/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{i=j}^{n}i^{-c}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}j^{1-c}-(n+1)^{1-c}&\text{if }c>1\,,\\
\log(n+1)-\log j&\text{if }c=1\,,\\
(n+1)^{1-c}-j^{1-c}&\text{if }c<1\,,\end{array}\right.

where
c=\min(b_{1}+a_{1}/2,~b_{2}+a_{2}/2)
.

Using the martingale property, Term (III) has variance

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{i})-\mathbb{E}U_{i}\right]\right\|_{2}^{2}=\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{n}M_{n,j}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|M_{n,j}\|_{2}^{2}\,,

whose upper bound depends on the value of
c
.

•

When
c>1
:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}(j^{1-c}-(n+1)^{1-c})^{2}\lesssim\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}n^{3-2c}&\text{if }c<3/2\\
\log n&\text{if }c\geq 3/2\,.\end{array}\right.

•

When
c=1
: using Stirling’s approximation,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\log(n+1)-\log j)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\log^{2}(n+1)-2\log(n+1)\sum_{j=1}^{n}\log j+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\log^{2}j

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle n\log^{2}n-2\log n(n\log n-n)+n\log^{2}n

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2n\log n\,.

•

When
c<1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}((n+1)^{1-c}-j^{1-c})^{2}\lesssim n^{3-2c}\,.

So in all cases we have

\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\mathbb{E}(U_{i}|{\mathcal{D}}_{i})-\mathbb{E}U_{i}\right]\right\|_{2}\lesssim n^{3/2-c}\sqrt{\log n}\,.

Putting together, we have the noise terms bounded by

\displaystyle(I)+(II)+(III)=O_{P}(n^{1/2-a_{2}/2}\vee\sqrt{\log n})+O_{P}(n^{3/2-c}\sqrt{\log n})\,.

On the other hand, the signal term is

\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\psi_{2,i}-\psi_{1,i})\gtrsim n^{1-a_{2}}\vee\log n\,.

Thus a sufficient condition for
\mathbb{P}(\Xi_{1,n}<\Xi_{2,n})\rightarrow 1
is
c>a_{2}+1/2
, which is equivalent to

\displaystyle b_{1}>

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+a_{2}-\frac{a_{1}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle b_{2}>

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+\frac{a_{2}}{2}\,.\qed

Remark 3.13

(Growing number of candidate models)
.

If we strengthen the stability condition to
\nabla_{j}\hat{f}^{(r)}_{i}(X_{i+1})
being
(i^{-b_{r}},\alpha)
-sub-Weibull, then we can derive exponential probability bounds of correct model selection. This would allow the number of candidate models to diverge sub-exponentially as the sample size grows. Online learning with a growing number of candidate models is naturally motivated by the need to gradually refine the tuning grid as the training sample size grows, which can be viewed as a sieve method on the regularity parameter space. However, such stability conditions will be harder to rigorously verify. In
Section

6
we provide further discussions and examples of stability bounds for some commonly used estimators such as stochastic gradient descent.

3.4
Weighted Rolling Validation

In the online model selection criterion (
25
) and the model quality assumption
3.10
, it is clear that the better model is defined through its superior asymptotic prediction risk. When reflected in the rolling validation risk
\Xi_{r,n}
, the expected model quality difference is

\mathbb{E}(\Xi_{1,n}-\Xi_{2,n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\psi_{1,i}-\psi_{2,i})\,.

Under
3.10
, this quantity diverges as
n
increases, and is driven by the contributions from large values of
i
. In other words, these assumptions suggest that the model quality is better reflected by the performance of
\hat{f}_{i}^{(r)}
at larger values of
i
. This intuition leads to the following weighted version of rolling validation (wRV).

\Xi_{r,n,\xi}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}i^{\xi}\ell(X_{i+1},\hat{f}_{i}^{(r)})\,,

(29)

where
\xi\geq 0
is a positive constant controlling the fraction of weight tilted towards the larger values of
i
.

The original rolling validation risk
\Xi_{r,n}
defined in (
28
) corresponds to the special case of
\Xi_{r,n,0}
. It is straightforward to check that the wRV risks are also fully compatible with the online learning protocol in
Definition

3.8
. More generally, we can write wRV as

\Xi_{r,n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\ell(X_{i+1},\hat{f}_{i}^{(r)})\,,

where
(w_{i}:i\geq 1)
is a non-decreasing sequence of positive weights such that

\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\frac{w_{i}}{\sum_{j\leq i}w_{j}}=0\,.

The specific choice of
w_{i}=i^{\xi}
is quite natural as a constant fraction of all sample points will have a constant fraction of the maximum weight. For example, at time
i
, a
(1-2^{-1/\xi})
proportion of sample points will have weights at least half as much as the maximum weight
i^{\xi}
. Plus these sample points are also the most recent ones, which are more representative of the asymptotic model quality than earlier sample points. In contrast, the exponential weighting
w_{i}=e^{\xi i}
will only assign non-trivial weights to a constant number of sample points, leading to an overly large variance in the wRV statistic.

Consistency of wRV.

Somewhat surprisingly, weighted rolling validation enjoys the same consistency properties, regardless of the value of
\xi
.

Corollary 3.14
.

Let
\Xi_{r,n,\xi}
be the wRV statistic defined in (
29
). Then, for any
\xi\geq 0
, under the same conditions as in
Theorem

3.12
, we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(\Xi_{1,n,\xi}<\Xi_{2,n,\xi})=1\,.

Intuitively, the weights
i^{\xi}
affect the variance and model quality gap in the same manner and will cancel out in the final signal-to-noise ratio calculation. The proof follows exactly the same procedure as that of
Theorem

3.12
.

Choosing
\xi
.

Although wRV can be consistent for any nonnegative
\xi
, the choice of
\xi
may still impact the finite sample bias-variance trade-off. In general, a small
\xi
leads to smaller variance but also larger bias (one way to interpret the bias is the representativeness of the wRV statistic for the asymptotic model quality). In practice, wRV with a positive
\xi
generally outperforms the original RV with
\xi=0
. Here we provide two ways to quantify the “bias” of wRV risk associated with the weight exponent
\xi
in a noiseless scenario.

Assume that the expected risks have the form
\psi_{1,i}=Ai^{-a}
and
\psi_{2,i}=Bi^{-b}
for positive constants
(A,a,B,b)
such that
0\leq b<a\leq 1
and
A>B
. Let
i^{*}=(A/B)^{1/(a-b)}
. Then
\psi_{1,i}>\psi_{2,i}
if
i<i^{*}
and
\psi_{1,i}<\psi_{2,i}
if
i\geq i^{*}
. We hope to have
\Xi_{1,i}<\Xi_{2,i}
soon after
i
exceeds
i^{*}
. In the noiseless case,
\Xi_{r,i,\xi}
reduces to its expected value, which equals, (when
r=1
)

\sum_{j=1}^{i}\psi_{1,j}=\sum_{j=1}^{i}Aj^{\xi-a}\approx\frac{A}{\xi+1-a}i^{\xi+1-a}\,,

provided that
\xi-a\neq-1
.
A similar equation can be derived for
r=2
. Thus, approximately, the noiseless wRV would be able to correctly favor the better model (
r=1
) when
i
is large enough such that

i\geq i(\xi)\coloneqq\left(\frac{A}{B}\right)^{\frac{1}{a-b}}\left(\frac{\xi+1-b}{\xi+1-a}\right)^{\frac{1}{a-b}}=i^{*}\left(1+\frac{a-b}{\xi+1-a}\right)^{\frac{1}{a-b}}\approx i^{*}\left(1+\frac{1}{\xi+1-a}\right)\,.

The quantity
1/(\xi+1-a)
in the above equation can be interpreted as the asymptotic
delay ratio
caused by the rolling sum. It is apparent that although the delay ratio is a monotone decreasing function of
\xi
, the improvement vanishes as
\xi
increases. In practice, a rule-of-thumb choice of
\xi
is

\xi_{\rm RoT}\equiv 1\,,

which controls the asymptotic delay ratio to be at most
1
, while keeping the variance at a reasonable level. Empirically, the model selection accuracy of wRV often observes the most significant improvement when
\xi
increases from
0
to
1
, and becomes relatively stable for
\xi\in[1,3]
.

3.5
Bibliographic Notes

The pairwise model selection consistency of
K
-fold CV under the least squares regression model is largely based on
Yang 2007
. Our
Theorem

3.3
corresponds to Theorem 1 in
Yang 2007
, with two notable distinctions and improvements. First, unlike Yang’s Theorem 1, which would require (in our notation)
\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{te}}}\psi_{2,n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\rightarrow\infty
, we only require
n_{\mathrm{te}}\Psi_{2,n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}\infty
, a
\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{te}}}
-factor weaker than Yang’s condition. This improvement is achieved by correctly tracking the exact level of variability of the excess loss functions given the fitted regression function in Equation (
21
). As shown in
Example

3.4
, our condition captures the correct sufficient condition in this simple example. Second, our result is stated without the “convergence at exact rate” condition, which is required in Yang’s Theorem 1. This simplification comes from a streamlined treatment. Earlier investigations of model selection consistency by cross-validation include
Zhang 1993
;
Shao 1993
.

The online model selection problem is motivated from recent developments in nonparametric stochastic approximation, such as kernel SGD
(
Kivinen et al. 2004
;
Dieuleveut and Bach 2016
)
and sieve SGD
(
Zhang and Simon 2022
;
Quan and Lin 2024
)
.
The rolling validation and weighted rolling validation results are based on
Zhang and Lei 2023
, which contains a detailed treatment of the diverging candidate set setting, as well as numerical examples demonstrating the practical advantage of wRV over traditional CV model selection.

Wang and Shen 2006
attempted to generalize the CV model selection consistency result in the form of
Yang 2007
to the setting of binary classification, requiring an accurate estimate of the conditional probability of
Y
given
X
used for a re-sampling step. In contrast, the proofs of the consistency results in this section mainly rely on the concentration of loss functions around their mean values. The general tools developed in
Section

2.3
,
Section

2.4
,
Section

2.5
could be used to provide useful results to establish similar model selection guarantees for general loss functions.

The model selection consistency of cross-validation has also been studied outside of the i.i.d. case. For example,
Racine 2000
developed a variant of cross-validation for consistent time series model selection. Some early explorations of methods similar to the weighted rolling validation in the time series forecasting setting include
Wei 1992
;
Ing 2007
.
In the network analysis literature,
Chen and Lei 2018
;
Li et al. 2020
;
Qin and Lei 2022
used different sample splitting and cross-validation techniques for community-structured network model selection.

4
Central Limit Theorems For Cross-Validation

The focus of this section shifts back to risk estimation, where the target of CV risk estimate
\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
, defined in (
2
), is either the average risk of the leave-one-fold-out estimates defined as
\bar{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
in (
3
) or its population version
\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}
defined in (
1
). Consistency and exponential concentration of
\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
around these targets have been established under various stability conditions in
Sections

2.3
,
2.4
and
2.5
. In this section we are interested in pushing these results one step further to establish corresponding central limit theorems.
These results, which are of independent theoretical interest, also pave the way for new methodological developments in a variety of inference problems, including constructing model selection confidence sets, which, as shown in
Section

5.2
, can be further developed into improved model selection methods that overcome the theoretical limitation of standard CV methods observed in
Section

3
.

4.1
CLT with Random Centering

We first consider the “random centering” case, where the quantity
\bar{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
is treated as the target of
\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
and hence the centering of the central limit theorem.
Proposition

2.7
and
Theorem

2.16
suggest that perturb-one stability of the loss function with rate
o(1/\sqrt{n})
implies sub-Gaussian type concentration of
\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
at rate
n^{-1/2}+o(1)
. In this section we further refine the analysis to show that under essentially the same stability condition the CV risk estimate centers at
\bar{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
with
\sqrt{n}
-asymptotic normality.

Our starting point is an intuition that if the risk function
\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
is perturb-one stable at a rate that vanishes faster than
1/\sqrt{n}
, then the variance contributed to
\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
by the fitting part
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
will be upper bounded by
o(1)
using the Efron-Stein inequality.

Theorem 4.1

(Efron-Stein Inequality)
.

Let
h
be a function that maps
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
to a real number, where
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}=(X_{i}:1\leq i\leq n)
consists of independent entries (not necessarily identically distributed or even defined in the same space). Then

\mathrm{Var}\left[h({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\nabla_{j}h\|_{2}^{2}\,.

The proof of Efron-Stein inequality is elementary, and is based on the telescope decomposition (
14
). A full proof can be found in Chapter 3.1 of
Boucheron et al. 2013
.

As a consequence, one can expect the summand term
\ell(X_{i},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})
in
\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
to behave like its de-randomized version

\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\ell(X_{i},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})|X_{i}\right]\,.

The subindex
n_{{\mathrm{tr}}}
in the notation
\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(\cdot)
emphasizes that this function only depends on the training sample size. More generally, we can define, for any
n\geq 1
,

\ell_{n}(x)=\mathbb{E}\left[\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})|X_{0}=x\right]\,,~~\text{and}~~\bar{\ell}_{n}(x)=\ell_{n}(x)-\mu_{n}\,.

(30)

The following result quantifies the contribution to the total variance of the loss function
\ell(X_{i},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})
from
{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}}
, i.e., the fitting randomness.

Proposition 4.2
.

Assume the fitting procedure is symmetric in the sense of
Definition

2.1
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
consists of i.i.d. sample points. Let
\epsilon_{n}=\|\nabla_{1}\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}

2
2

2

By symmetry of
\hat{f}
and the i.i.d. assumption, the particular choice of
i
in
\nabla_{i}\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
does not matter as long as
i\in[n]
. Then

\mathrm{Var}[\ell_{n}(X_{0})]\leq\mathrm{Var}[\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})]\leq\mathrm{Var}[\ell_{n}(X_{0})]+\frac{n}{2}\epsilon_{n}^{2}\,.

Proof of
Proposition

4.2
.

Using the variance decomposition

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}[\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})]=\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathbb{E}(\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})|X_{0})\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{Var}(\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})|X_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left[\ell_{n}(X_{0})\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{Var}(\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})|X_{0})\right]\,.

Thus it suffices to show

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{Var}(\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})|X_{0})\right]\leq(n/2)\|\nabla_{1}\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}^{2}\,.

Using the Efron-Stein inequality on the conditional distribution of
\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
given
X_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})|X_{0})\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbb{E}\left[(\nabla_{i}\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n}))^{2}|X_{0}\right]\,.

The desired result follows by taking expected value on both sides.
∎

Proposition

4.2
suggests that if the loss function is stable enough then the randomness in
\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
comes mostly from
X_{0}
and it is reasonable to expect

\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}\approx\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})

(31)

which is the average of
n
i.i.d. random variables. However, (
31
) may not be accurate enough in general cases for the purpose of Gaussian approximation. We will need a slight modification on the LHS so that it focuses on the randomness contributed from the loss function evaluation, as detailed in the following theorem.

Theorem 4.3

(CV-CLT, Random Centering)
.

Let
(X_{i}:i\geq 0)
be an i.i.d. sequence of sample points in
{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}=\{X_{i}:1\leq i\leq n\}
.
Let
\hat{f}:\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes n}\mapsto\mathcal{F}
be a symmetric estimation procedure,
\ell(\cdot,\cdot)
a loss function such that

\|\nabla_{1}\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=o(\sigma_{n}/\sqrt{n})\,,

(32)

where
\sigma_{n}^{2}=\mathrm{Var}\left[\ell_{n}(X_{0})\right]\in(0,\infty)
and

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\bar{\ell}_{n}(X_{0})}{\sigma_{n}}\right)^{2}\mathds{1}\left(\frac{|\bar{\ell}_{n}(X_{0})|}{\sigma_{n}}\geq\epsilon\sqrt{n}\right)=0

(33)

for all
\epsilon>0
and
\ell_{n}(X_{0})
,
\bar{\ell}_{n}(X_{0})
defined in (
30
).
Then for any value
K
, the
K
-fold cross-validated risk
\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
satisfies

\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}}\left(\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\bar{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}\right)\rightsquigarrow N(0,1)\,,

where the symbol “
\rightsquigarrow
” means “convergence in distribution”, and
\bar{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
is defined in (
3
).

Proof of
Theorem

4.3
.

The proof proceeds by rigorously establishing a modified version of the “approximate linearity” of
\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
in (
31
).

Let
\xi_{i}=\ell(X_{i},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})-R({\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})
and
\bar{\xi}_{i}=\mathbb{E}\left[\xi_{i}|X_{i}\right]=\bar{\ell}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})
.

The rest of the proof consists of two main steps. In the first step we show that

\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}}\left(\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\bar{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\xi}_{i}=o_{P}(1)\,.

(34)

In fact, by construction, the LHS of
Equation

34
multiplied by
\sqrt{n}\sigma_{n_{t}r}
equals

\displaystyle n\left(\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\bar{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\xi}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i})\,,

whose squared
L_{2}
norm is

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i})^{2}+\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\mathbb{E}(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i})(\xi_{j}-\bar{\xi}_{j})\,.

(35)

For the squared term
(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i})^{2}
we have, using the same argument as in
Proposition

4.2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i})^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{Var}(\xi_{i}|X_{i})\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j\notin I_{k_{i}}}\mathbb{E}((\nabla_{j}\xi_{i})^{2}|X_{i})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\notin I_{k_{i}}}\|\nabla_{j}\xi_{i}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\notin I_{k_{i}}}\|\nabla_{j}\ell(X_{i},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})\|_{2}^{2}=o(\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2})\,,

where the second inequality follows from the fact

\|\nabla_{j}\xi_{i}\|_{2}\leq\|\nabla_{j}\ell(X_{i},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})\|_{2}

as implied by
Lemma

4.17
in
Section

4.4
. As a result, the total contribution from the squared terms in (
35
) is
o(n\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2})
.

For the cross term
(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i})(\xi_{j}-\bar{\xi}_{j})
, if
k_{i}=k_{j}
, then by conditioning on
{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}}
and using conditional independence of
X_{i}
and
X_{j}
given
{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}}
we have

\mathbb{E}(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i})(\xi_{j}-\bar{\xi}_{j})=0
. Now we only need to control the cross-term contributed by
(i,j)
pairs such that
j\notin I_{k_{i}}
. The main trick here is the “free nabla” lemma (
Lemma

4.16
in
Section

4.4
). By applying
Lemma

4.16
twice we have, for
j\notin I_{k_{i}}

\mathbb{E}(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i})(\xi_{j}-\bar{\xi}_{j})=\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{j}(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i})][\nabla_{i}(\xi_{j}-\bar{\xi}_{j})]

whose absolute value is
o(\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}/n_{\mathrm{tr}})
. Thus the total contribution from the cross-terms in (
35
) is
o(n\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2})
. This establishes (
34
).

It remains to show that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\xi}_{i}\rightsquigarrow N(0,1)\,,

which, under (
33
), follows from the standard Lindeberg central limit theorem for triangular arrays
(
Durrett 2019
, Theorem 3.4.5)
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

The proof of
Theorem

4.3
implies that

d_{W,1}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}}\left(\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\bar{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}\right),~N(0,1)\right)\rightarrow 0\,,

where
d_{W,1}
is the Wasserstein-1 distance.
Moreover, if we assume a specific rate of convergence in (
32
), as well as a stronger integrability condition than (
33
) such as uniform boundedness of
\|[\ell_{n}(X_{0})-\mu_{n}]/\sigma_{n}\|_{3}
, then we can establish Berry-Esseen type finite sample error bound of normal approximation in the Wasserstein-1 and Kolmogorov-Smirnov distances.

Remark 4.5
.

In most applications, it is reasonable to assume that there exists a pair of positive constants
(\underline{c},\bar{c})
such that

0<\underline{c}^{2}\leq\mathrm{Var}[\ell_{n}(X_{0})]\leq\|\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}^{2}\leq\bar{c}^{2}<\infty

(36)

for all
n
. For example, in regression problems, the lower bound corresponds to the aleatoric uncertainty that comes from the observation noise, and cannot be reduced by the prediction function. The upper bound usually corresponds the parameter space and the total variability of the random variables.

As another consequence of
Proposition

4.2
, one can expect to obtain a consistent estimate of
\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}
using the sample variance of
\{\ell(X_{i},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}}):i\in[n]\}
since the variance is contributed mostly by the evaluation point
X_{i}
.

Theorem 4.6

(Variance Estimation)
.

Let

\hat{\sigma}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\ell(X_{i},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})-\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}\right)^{2}.

In addition to the same condition as in
Theorem

4.3
, assume that

[\bar{\ell}_{n}(X_{0})/\sigma_{n}]^{2}
is uniformly integrable.
Then

\frac{\hat{\sigma}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}}{\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}1\,.

The additional uniform integrability assumption ensures that the sample variance of
\{\bar{\ell}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i}):1\leq i\leq n\}
is a consistent estimate of its population version. It can be satisfied under moment conditions, such as uniformly bounded
\|\bar{\ell}_{n}(X_{0})/\sigma_{n}\|_{2+\delta}
for any
\delta>0
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.6
.

Define

\tilde{\sigma}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[\bar{\ell}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})]^{2}\,,

which, under uniform integrability of
[\bar{\ell}_{n}(X_{0})/\sigma_{n}]^{2}
, satisfies

\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}}{\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}1\,.

It suffices to show that
\hat{\sigma}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}=o_{P}(\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2})
.
By construction, we have

\displaystyle|\hat{\sigma}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}-\tilde{\sigma}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}|=

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\ell(X_{i},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})-\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})+\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\right]\right.

\displaystyle~~\left.\times\left[\ell(X_{i},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})-\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})+\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}+2(\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})\right]\right|\,.

Using Jensen’s inequality and the same argument as bounding
\|\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}
in the proof of
Theorem

4.3
we obtain

\|\ell(X_{i},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})-\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})\|_{2}=o(\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})\,.

Following the proof of
Theorem

2.16
, we can obtain, under the stability condition (
32
),

\|\nabla_{i}\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}\|_{2}=o(\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}/\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})

which, by Efron-Stein, further implies that

\|\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\|_{2}=o(\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})\,.

Then Cauchy-Schwartz and triangle inequality imply that

\|\tilde{\sigma}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}-\hat{\sigma}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}\|_{1}=o(\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2})\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

4.2
CLT with Deterministic Centering

In this subsection we develop a central limit theorem for the CV risk estimate with the deterministic centering:
\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}=\mathbb{E}\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
.

The random centering CLT proved in the previous subsection provides a head start. A key intermediate step in that proof is, under the loss stability condition (
32
) and recalling that
\bar{\ell}_{n}(x)=\ell_{n}(x)-\mu_{n}
,

\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\bar{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\ell}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})+o_{P}(1/\sqrt{n})\,,

which can be re-written equivalently as

\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\ell}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})+\frac{1}{n}G+o_{P}(1/\sqrt{n})\,,

(37)

where

G=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[R({\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\right]=\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{\mathrm{te}}\left[R({\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k})-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\right]

(38)

accounts for the difference between the random centering
\bar{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
and the deterministic centering
\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}
.

The additional challenge in order to establish a deterministic centering CLT for
\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
is to characterize the randomness in
G
, a mean zero random variable that involves each sample point in a symmetric fashion.
Unlike the loss function, where each sample point
X_{i}
is evaluated exactly once in
\ell(X_{i},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})
, the randomness in
G
features the sum of the risks of parameters fitted from different leave-one-fold-out subsamples, and each
X_{i}
contributes, in a symmetric fashion as other fitting sample points, to all but one such risks. Thus we will proceed by considering the projection of
G
onto each
X_{i}
, which is involved in the parameter fitting part in
R({\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{j}})
for all
j\notin I_{k_{i}}
. Define

g_{n}(x)\coloneqq n\mathbb{E}\left[R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})|X_{1}=x\right]\,,~~~~\text{and}~~~~\bar{g}_{n}(x)=g_{n}(x)-n\mu_{n}\,.

(39)

The quantity
g_{n}(X_{i})
is the counterpart of
\ell_{n}(X_{i})
when considering the contribution of
X_{i}
to the
fitting variability
.

By construction, it is straightforward to check that

\bar{g}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})=\mathbb{E}(G|X_{i})\,.

It is reasonable to expect the linear decomposition

G\approx\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{g}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})\,.

(40)

If we can establish (
40
) with enough level of accuracy, then one can establish a CLT with deterministic centering from (
37
) using standard i.i.d. sums.

To further explore the plausibility of (
40
), we consider the following martingale decomposition of
G
:

G=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(G|{\mathcal{D}}_{i})-\mathbb{E}(G|{\mathcal{D}}_{i-1})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}(\nabla_{i}G|{\mathcal{D}}_{i})

(41)

with the convention
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}=\emptyset
.

The martingale increment
\mathbb{E}(\nabla_{i}G|{\mathcal{D}}_{i})
represents the contribution to
G
from the
i
th sample point. We shall also see that, at least intuitively, when the loss function is stable, the choice of ordering of
i
in (
41
) does not matter. Now it seems natural to expect this increment to be close to
\bar{g}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})
. Indeed we have, using another round of martingale decomposition with respect to the sigma fields generated by
({\mathcal{D}}_{j},X_{i})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\nabla_{i}G|{\mathcal{D}}_{i})=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\nabla_{i}G|X_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\left[\mathbb{E}(\nabla_{i}G|{\mathcal{D}}_{j},X_{i})-\mathbb{E}(\nabla_{i}G|{\mathcal{D}}_{j-1},X_{i})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\mathbb{E}(\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}G|{\mathcal{D}}_{j},X_{i})

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})+\Lambda_{i}\,.

(42)

Now we arrive at the following approximate linear form for
G
:

G=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{g}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{i}\,.

(43)

It remains to control the residual term
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{i}
. Two observations are useful.

1.

The sequence
(\Lambda_{i}:1\leq i\leq n)
is a martingale increment sequence with respect to the filtration corresponding to
({\mathcal{D}}_{i}:1\leq i\leq n)
.

2.

For each
i
, we have

\|\Lambda_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\leq(i-1)n^{2}\max_{1\leq j<l\leq n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\|\nabla_{j}\nabla_{l}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})\|_{2}^{2}\,.

(44)

To prove (
44
), using the martingale decomposition of
\Lambda_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{i}\|_{2}^{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\|\mathbb{E}(\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}G|{\mathcal{D}}_{j},X_{i})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\|\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}G\|_{2}^{2}\,.

But
\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}G=\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{\mathrm{te}}\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k})
and observe that
\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k})\neq 0
only if
i,j\notin I_{k}
. Thus
\|\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}G\|_{2}\leq n\max_{1\leq j<l\leq n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\|\nabla_{j}\nabla_{l}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})\|_{2}
.

The above derivation essentially proves the following central limit theorem for CV risk estimates with deterministic centering.

Theorem 4.7

(CV-CLT, Deterministic Centering)
.

Let
(X_{i}:0\leq i<\infty)
be a sequence of i.i.d. sample points in
{\mathcal{X}}
,
\hat{f}:\bigcup_{n\geq 1}{\mathcal{X}}^{\otimes n}\mapsto\mathcal{F}
a symmetric estimation procedure, and
\ell:{\mathcal{X}}\times\mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
a loss function. Let
K
be an integer that evenly divides
n
and allowed to change with
n
. Suppose

\tau_{n}^{2}\coloneqq\mathrm{Var}(\ell_{n}(X_{0})+g_{n}(X_{0}))>0

for all
n

and the following stability condition holds

1.

For any
1\leq i\leq n
,
\|\nabla_{i}\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=o(\tau_{n}/\sqrt{n})
;

2.

For any
1\leq j<i\leq n
,
\|\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=o(\tau_{n}n^{-3/2})
.

3.

For any
\epsilon>0
,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\bar{\ell}_{n}(X_{0})+\bar{g}_{n}(X_{0})}{\tau_{n}}\right)^{2}\mathds{1}\left(\left|\frac{\bar{\ell}_{n}(X_{0})+\bar{g}_{n}(X_{0})}{\tau_{n}}\right|\geq\epsilon\sqrt{n}\right)=0\,.

(45)

Then

\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}}\left(\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\right)\rightsquigarrow N(0,1)\,.

Proof of
Theorem

4.7
.

According to the proof of
Theorem

4.3
, the first stability condition establishes (
37
). Now the second stability condition ensures that
n^{-1/2}\sum_{i}\Lambda_{i}=o_{P}(\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})
according to (
44
). The result then follows from the standard Lindeberg CLT for the triangular array
\{\bar{\ell}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})+\bar{g}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i}):1\leq i\leq n\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
.
∎

The asymptotic variance
\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}
is the total variance of the sum of two parts:
\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})
and
g_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})
, which corresponds to the evaluation and estimation randomness contributed by the sample point
X_{i}
, respectively. In contrast, the random centering CLT
Theorem

4.3
only captures the evaluation part involving
\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})
, leaving the randomness of estimation to the random centering
\bar{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
. While it is relatively straightforward to assume the boundedness of
\mathrm{Var}(\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1}))
, the behavior of
\mathrm{Var}(\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1})+g_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1}))
is harder to track in general. Intuitively,
\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1})
and
g_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1})
should have a weak positive correlation, because if
X_{1}
is a “good sample point” then it should reduce the loss function as well as the risk of the estimate obtained using it as one of the training sample points. This makes a positive constant lower bound of
\mathrm{Var}(\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1})+g_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1}))
more or less reasonable. In order to assume an upper bound on
\mathrm{Var}(g_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1}))
, it helps to consider the variability of
n_{\mathrm{tr}}\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})
, because there are
n_{\mathrm{tr}}
terms in
G
that involves
X_{1}
. An upper bound on
\mathrm{Var}[g_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1})]
can be obtained through the first-order risk stability
\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(g_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1}))=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left[n_{\mathrm{tr}}\mathbb{E}(R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})|X_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Var}\left[n_{\mathrm{tr}}\nabla_{1}\mathbb{E}(R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})|X_{1})\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}n_{\mathrm{tr}}^{2}\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})\|_{2}^{2}\,.

(46)

In order for the upper bound in (
4.2
) to be
O(1)
, it would require a risk stability rate of
O_{P}(1/n_{\mathrm{tr}})
, which seems substantially more stringent than the corresponding rate assumed for the loss stability in (
32
) as well as Part 1 of the stability assumption in
Theorem

4.7
. We argue that in many machine learning contexts, the risk can have much better stability than the loss function itself.

As a first example, consider
X_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}N(0,1)
and
\ell(x,{\mathcal{D}}_{n})=(x-\hat{\mu}_{n})^{2}
where
\hat{\mu}_{n}
is the empirical mean of
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
. It is straightforward to check that
\|\nabla_{1}\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}\asymp n^{-1}
but
\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}\asymp n^{-3/2}
.

More generally, suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a Hilbert space, and we have the first order approximation

\nabla_{i}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\approx\left\langle\left(\frac{dR}{df}\bigg|_{f=\hat{f}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})}\right)\,,~\left(\nabla_{i}\hat{f}\right)\right\rangle\,.

Usually
\nabla_{i}\hat{f}
corresponds to the stability of the loss function
\ell(X_{0};\hat{f}({\mathcal{D}}_{n}))
if
\ell(X_{0},f)
is smooth in
f
. For risk minimization estimators, it is typically the case that
\frac{dR(f)}{df}\big|_{f=\hat{f}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})}\approx 0
. Thus for such approximate risk minimization estimators it is reasonable to expect
R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})
to be much more stable than
\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
. This intuition also helps explaining the condition
\|\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=o(n^{-3/2})
required in
Theorem

4.7
. We will resume this discussion after presenting the variance estimation result below. Further discussion and examples of stable algorithms are presented in
Section

6
, especially in
Section

6.3
for the risk stability.

Variance Estimation with Deterministic Centering

It is unclear how to construct a consistent estimate of the asymptotic variance of
\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
without auxiliary sample points that are not in
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
. Now suppose we do have access to some auxiliary sample points, independently drawn from the same distribution. The question we seek to answer here is how many such auxiliary sample points would be sufficient to guarantee a consistent estimate of the asymptotic variance of the CV risk estimate with deterministic centering.

If we have
\omega(n)
auxiliary sample points, then we can generate
\omega(1)
i.i.d. copies of
\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
and then obtain a consistent variance estimate from these i.i.d. copies. Now we show that one can also obtain a consistent variance estimate using only
\omega(1)
auxiliary sample points.

We begin with the proof of
Theorem

4.7
, which is based on the following more refined version of (
37
) and (
40
) with an explicit expression of the residual,

\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\bar{\ell}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})+\bar{g}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})\right]+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{i}\,,

where
\xi_{i}=\ell(X_{i},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})-R({\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{i}})
,
\bar{\xi}_{i}=\mathbb{E}(\xi_{i}|X_{i})=\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}
, and
\Lambda_{i}=\mathbb{E}(G|{\mathcal{D}}_{i})-\mathbb{E}(G|{\mathcal{D}}_{i-1})
.

To study the variance of
\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
, it is natural to consider
\nabla_{i}\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
.
In particular we will consider
i=n
, and, assuming that
k_{n}=K
,

n\nabla_{n}\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}=\nabla_{n}(\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{n})+g_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{n}))+\sum_{i\notin I_{K}}\nabla_{n}(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i})+\nabla_{n}(\xi_{n}-\bar{\xi}_{n})+\nabla_{n}\Lambda_{n}\,.

(47)

The convenience of choosing
i=n
in
\nabla_{i}
is reflected in the last term of the equation above, where all
(\Lambda_{i}:i<n)
disappear under the
\nabla_{n}
operator. It is important to note that (
47
) also holds for arbitrary index
i\in[n]
, with the last term becoming
\nabla_{i}\Lambda_{i,n}
, where
\Lambda_{i,n}
is the counterpart of
\Lambda_{n}
when one swaps index
i
and index
n
in the martingale decompositions in (
41
) and (
42
).

The first term in the RHS (
47
) gives us the desired i.i.d. random variable with the desired asymptotic variance. Thus, if we can show that the other terms in the RHS of (
47
) are all sufficiently small with vanishing
L_{2}
norms, then one can use the empirical variance of
n\nabla_{i}\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
with
\omega(1)
many distinct
i
’s, which would only require
\omega(1)
auxiliary random sample.

Among the residual terms in the RHS of (
47
), the most non-trivial one to control is the second term

\nabla_{n}\sum_{i\notin I_{K}}(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i})
. In the proof of
Theorem

4.3
we have shown

\left\|\sum_{i\notin I_{K}}(\xi_{i}-\bar{\xi}_{i})\right\|_{2}=o(\sqrt{n}\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})\,,

thus we only need the
\nabla_{n}
operator to cancel out the additional
\sqrt{n}
factor in this upper bound. While this is intuitively plausible, we will need some additional assumptions for a rigorous result. We consider the following.

Assumption 4.8
.

(a)

\|\nabla_{1}\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=o((nK)^{-1/2}\tau_{n})
.

(b)

The loss function satisfies
\|\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=o(n^{-1}\tau_{n})
.

The two parts of
4.8
represent different possible strengthening of the stability condition (
32
). Part (a) is easy to hold when the number of folds
K
is a constant or grows with
n
at a slow rate. When
K
is a constant, Part (a) of
4.8
is equivalent to the original stability condition (
32
). On the other hand, part (b) of
4.8
requires a “second-order stability”. Intuitively, if applying the perturb-one difference operator
\nabla_{i}
reduces the magnitude of
\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
by a factor of
o(1/\sqrt{n})
, then applying two such operators will reduce the magnitude by a factor of
o(1/n)
. The advantage of Part (b) is that it can potentially cover the case of large values of
K
, such as
K=n
. But such an advantage comes at the cost of having to verify the second-order stability condition. We will discuss examples of second-order stability in
Section

6.2
.

Formally, assume we have auxiliary samples
X_{1}^{\prime},...,X_{n_{\mathrm{aux}}}^{\prime}
independent of
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
with sample size
n_{\mathrm{aux}}=\omega(1)
. Consider the following pseudo-estimate of the asymptotic variance of the CV risk with deterministic centering.

\hat{\tau}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2n_{\mathrm{aux}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{aux}}}(n\nabla_{i}\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K})^{2}\,.

(48)

Theorem 4.9
.

Under the assumptions of
Theorem

4.7
, assuming in addition that either part (a) or part (b) of
4.8
holds, if the auxiliary sample size
n_{\mathrm{aux}}
satisfies

\frac{\|\bar{\ell}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1})+\bar{g}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1})\|_{4}^{4}}{n_{\mathrm{aux}}\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{4}}\rightarrow 0\,,

(49)

we have

\frac{\hat{\tau}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}}{\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}1\,.

If
\|\bar{\ell}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1})+\bar{g}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1})\|_{4}=O(\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})
, then
Theorem

4.9
implies consistent estimation with
n_{\mathrm{aux}}=\omega(1)
. In practice, we can hold out
n_{\mathrm{aux}}=\sqrt{n}
for variance estimation and conduct cross-validation on the remaining sample points.

Proof of
Theorem

4.9
.

Using (
47
), we have

n\nabla_{i}\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}=\nabla_{i}[\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})+g_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})]+\Delta_{i}

where
\Delta_{i}
equals the sum of the last three terms in the RHS of (
47
)
3
3

3

Although (
47
) is derived for
i=n
, the validity of this decomposition for all
i\in[n]
is due to the symmetry, as discussed in the text after (
47
).
.
Let
W_{i}=\nabla_{i}[\ell_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})+g_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})]
. Then

\frac{\hat{\tau}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}}{\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}}-1=\frac{\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{aux}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{aux}}}(W_{i}^{2}/2-\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2})}{\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}}+\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{aux}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{aux}}}\frac{W_{i}\Delta_{i}}{\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}}+\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{aux}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{aux}}}\frac{\Delta_{i}^{2}}{2\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}}\,.

The second and third terms on the RHS of the above equation has
L_{1}
norm bounded by
o(1)
because
\|W_{i}/\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\|_{2}=\sqrt{2}
and
\|\Delta_{i}/\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\|_{2}=o(1)
uniformly over
i
according to
Lemma

4.19
. Thus it suffices to show the first term is
o_{P}(1)
. In fact,
the first term has mean
0
and variance

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Var}(W_{1})}{4n_{\mathrm{aux}}\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{4}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\|\bar{\ell}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1})+\bar{g}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1})\|_{4}^{4}}{n_{\mathrm{aux}}\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{4}}=o(1)\,.\qed

Revisiting the Risk Stability Conditions

The variance estimation argument above also provides further insights to the asymptotic variance
\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}
and the risk stability condition in
Theorem

4.7
. To begin with, we repeat the derivation of (
41
), (
42
), (
43
), (
44
), and (
47
) for the risk
R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})
. In particular, (
43
) becomes

R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\mathbb{E}(R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})|X_{i})-\mu\right]+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\Lambda}_{i}

where
\tilde{\Lambda}_{i}
is the counterpart of
\Lambda_{i}
in (
43
).
Also (
47
) becomes (suppose we put
i=1
in the final position in the first martingale decomposition (
41
))

\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})=\nabla_{1}\mathbb{E}(R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})|X_{1})+\nabla_{1}\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}\,,

(50)

where
\|\nabla_{1}\tilde{\Lambda}_{1}\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\|\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})\|_{2}
by a similar argument used in (
44
).

Therefore, we have the following result on the relationship between the variances of
\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})
and
g_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{1})
, which provides the reverse direction of (
4.2
).

Lemma 4.10
.

If
\mathrm{Var}(R({\mathcal{D}}_{n}))>0
and

\sqrt{n}\|\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=o(\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2})\,,

(51)

then

\frac{\mathrm{Var}(g_{n}(X_{1}))}{n^{2}\mathrm{Var}(\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n}))}\rightarrow\frac{1}{2}\,.

Proof of
Lemma

4.10
.

Equation (
4.2
) shows that the ratio is upper bounded by
1/2
. On the other hand, recall that
g_{n}(X_{1})=n\mathbb{E}[R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})|X_{1}]
and (
50
) implies that

\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2}n^{-1}\|\bar{g}_{n}(X_{1})\|_{2}+o(\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2})
, which provides the other direction.
∎

Putting things together we can have the following more refined view of
Theorem

4.7
as well as its relationship with
Theorem

4.3
.

Proposition 4.11

(CV-CLT, Consolidated Version)
.

Assuming an i.i.d. sequence of
X_{i}:i\geq 0
and a symmetric loss function
\ell(\cdot,\cdot)
.

1.

If
n\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=o(\sigma_{n})
, then

\frac{\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}}{\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\rightarrow}}1

and (
32
), (
33
) imply that

\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}}(\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})\rightsquigarrow N(0,1)\,.

2.

If
n\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=\Omega(\sigma_{n})
and
\tau_{n}=\Omega(\sigma_{n})
, then

\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}}(\hat{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})\rightsquigarrow N(0,1)\,.

provided that
\sqrt{n}\|\nabla_{i}\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=o(\tau_{n})
and that (
51
), (
45
) hold.

Proof of
Proposition

4.11
.

For the first part, it suffices to show that
\|\bar{R}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\|_{2}=o(\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}/\sqrt{n})
, which is in turn implied by

\|R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\|_{2}=o(\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}/\sqrt{n})
using symmetry among the folds and triangle inequality. The condition
n\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=o(\sigma_{n})
, combined with Efron-Stein, guarantees
\|R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})-\mu_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\|_{2}=o(\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}/\sqrt{n})
.

For the second part, it suffices to show that the conditions in
Theorem

4.7
are satisfied. In particular, we only need to verify the second order risk stability in
Theorem

4.7
. First,
Lemma

4.10
ensures that
\|g_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{0})\|_{2}\asymp n_{\mathrm{tr}}\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})\|_{2}
. The additional assumptions
n\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=\Omega(\sigma_{n})
and
\tau_{n}=\Omega(\sigma_{n})
ensure that
\tau_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\asymp n_{\mathrm{tr}}\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})\|_{2}
. Thus the second order risk stability condition (part 2) of
Theorem

4.7
is satisfied because by (
51
) we have
\|\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}\leq n^{-1/2}\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=o(n^{-3/2}\tau_{n})
.
∎

Proposition

4.11
provides a more transparent view of the role played by the risk stability quantities
\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}
and
\|\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}
in the CLT with deterministic centering. In the first part of
Proposition

4.11
, the condition
n\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=o(\sigma_{n})
indicates that the fitting randomness in
nR({\mathcal{D}}_{n})
contributed from a single sample point
X_{1}
has much smaller variability than the evaluation
\ell_{n}(X_{1})
. In this case the second order risk stability
\|\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}
is irrelevant and the deterministic centering essentially behaves like the random centering.

In the second part of
Proposition

4.11
, the condition
n\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=\Omega(\sigma_{n})
means that the variability in the fitting is at least as large as that in the evaluation. The condition
\tau_{n}=\Omega(\sigma_{n})
ensures that these two sources of variability do not cancel each other when we add up
\ell_{n}(X_{1})
and
g_{n}(X_{1})
. As mentioned in the discussion after
Theorem

4.7
, it is often plausible to assume a nonnegative correlation between
\ell_{n}(X_{1})
and
g_{n}(X_{1})
, and hence no cancellation of variance. In this case, the key condition on the second order risk stability is that
\|\nabla_{1}\nabla_{2}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}
is much smaller than
n^{-1/2}\|\nabla_{1}R(D_{n})\|_{2}
. So this is analogous to the original loss stability condition (
32
) in the sense that we obtain an
o(n^{-1/2})
factor from applying one more
\nabla
-operator. Moreover, if
n\|\nabla_{1}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}\gg\sigma_{n}
, the requirement of
\sqrt{n}\|\nabla_{1}\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})\|_{2}=o(\tau_{n})
is also weaker than (
32
) because the fitting variability dominates and we can have a higher tolerance of the instability.

4.3
High-Dimensional Gaussian Comparison

The central limit theorems developed in
Section

4.1
and
Section

4.2
are concerned with a single estimator
\hat{f}
. In the context of model selection and model comparison, it is necessary to study the simultaneous fluctuation of the CV risk estimates of multiple estimators. While the extension of the central limit theorem to multivariate cases with a finite and fixed number of estimators is straightforward, in this subsection we consider the situation with a potentially large number of estimators.

To begin with, we first introduce a high-dimensional Gaussian comparison result for sums of independent random vectors, initiated by
Chernozhukov et al. 2013
.
A typical form of high-dimensional Gaussian comparison is to compare the distribution of the maximum of a random vector to that of a Gaussian vector with matching mean and variance.
For a vector
z=(z_{r}:1\leq r\leq m)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, let
\max z=\max_{r\in[m]}z_{r}
be the value of the maximum entry of
z
.
The following result is a simplified version of Theorem 2.5 of
Chernozhukov et al. 2022
adapted for our setting.

Theorem 4.12

(Gaussian Approximation, Simplified i.i.d. Case)
.

Let
(\mathbf{Z}_{i}:i\in[n])
be i.i.d. centered random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that each coordinate of
\mathbf{Z}_{1}
has unit variance.
Let
\mathbf{W}_{n}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{Z}_{i}
and
\mathbf{Y}_{n}\sim N(0,\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{Z}_{1}\mathbf{Z}_{1}^{T}))
.

Let
\|\max|\mathbf{Z}_{1}|\|_{4}=B_{n}
and assume

\max_{1\leq r\leq m}\|Z_{1,r}\|_{4}\leq\kappa
for a positive constant
\kappa
not depending on
(n,m)
, then for any
t\in\mathbb{R}

\left|\mathbb{P}(\max\mathbf{W}_{n}\leq t)-\mathbb{P}(\max\mathbf{Y}_{n}\leq t)\right|\leq C_{\kappa}\frac{B_{n}(\log m)^{5/4}}{n^{1/4}}\,,

(52)

for a constant
C_{\kappa}
depending on
\kappa
only.

Gaussian comparison results like this provide probability-theoretic foundations for high-dimensional inference tasks, such as construction of simultaneous confidence intervals for high dimensional mean vectors.

When studying the joint randomness of the CV risk estimates of
m
estimators, it is more convenient to use vector notations. Let
\bm{\ell}
be the vector version of
(\ell^{(r)}:1\leq r\leq m)
. Define
\bm{\ell}_{n}
,
\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
, and
\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
, correspondingly.
Let
S_{n}
be the
m\times m
diagonal matrix with the
r
th diagonal entry being
\sigma_{n}^{(r)}\coloneqq\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(\ell_{n}^{(r)}(X_{0}))}
, and
\Gamma_{n}
the correlation matrix of
\bm{\ell}_{n}(X_{0})
:

\Gamma_{n}(r,s)={\rm Corr}\left[\ell_{n}^{(r)}(X_{0}),~\ell_{n}^{(s)}(X_{0})\right]\,.

(53)

Our goal is to characterize the simultaneous fluctuation of
\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
as an
m
-dimensional random vector.

Theorem

4.3
implies that the
m
-dimensional vector
\sqrt{n}S_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{-1}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K})

is entry-wise asymptotically standard normal, provided that, among other regularity conditions, the loss stability condition (
32
) holds for each estimator
\hat{f}^{(r)}
. Therefore, it is natural to consider the corresponding multivariate Gaussian vector
\mathbf{Y}_{n}\sim N(0,\Gamma_{n})
as the target in the high-dimensional Gaussian comparison.

More specifically:
When does (
52
) hold when
\mathbf{W}_{n}
is replaced by

\sqrt{n}S_{n}^{-1}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K})
and
\mathbf{Y}_{n}\sim N(0,\Gamma_{n})
?

The following result provides a positive answer to this question under a strengthened version stability condition (
32
).

Theorem 4.13
.

Let
(X_{i}:i\geq 0)
be a sequence of i.i.d. sample points, and
(\hat{f}^{(r)}:r\in[m])
a collection of symmetric estimators. Assume

1.

there exist a positive constant
\alpha
and a sequence
\epsilon_{n}
such that, for all
r\in[m]
and all
n
,

\frac{\sqrt{nK}}{\sigma_{n}^{(r)}}\nabla_{1}\ell^{(r)}(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})\text{ is }(\epsilon_{n},\alpha)\text{-SW}\,,

(54)

2.

and there exists a finite positive constant
\kappa
such that for all
r
and
n

\frac{\bar{\ell}_{n}^{(r)}(X_{0})}{\sigma_{n}^{(r)}}~~\text{is }(\kappa,\alpha)\text{-SW}\,.

(55)

If the number of models
m=m_{n}
satisfies

\frac{(\log m)^{4\alpha+5}}{n}=o(1)\,,~~\text{and}~~(\log m)^{\alpha+3/2}\epsilon_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}=o(1),

(56)

then we have, for
Y_{n}\sim N(0,\Gamma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}})
with
\Gamma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}
defined in (
53
),

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\max\sqrt{n}S_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{-1}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K})\leq t\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\max\mathbf{Y}_{n}\leq t\right)\right|=o(1)\,.

(57)

In
Theorem

4.13
, we allow the number of models
m=m_{n}
to vary with
n
. Equations (
54
) and (
55
) are counterparts of (
32
) and (
33
) respectively. The sub-Weibull tail enables simultaneous upper bound on many residual terms. The factor of
\sqrt{K}
on the LHS of
eq.

54
is necessary here because the “free nabla trick” used in the proof of
Theorem

4.3
no longer works for general
L_{q}
norms and we can only use Rio’s inequality within each test sample fold and resort to triangle inequality to combine the folds.
Condition (
54
) is less stringent for smaller values of
K
, and becomes comparable to (
32
) if
K=O(1)
.
In practice,
K
-fold CV with a constant
K
, such as
K=5
and
K=10
, is perhaps the most common version.

Remark 4.14
.

Theorem

4.13
is stated for the maximum entry of the marginally standardized error vector, which corresponds to one-sided simultaneous confidence bound. The same result can be easily extended to a two-sided version in that (
57
) holds true, under the same conditions, for the corresponding vectors of absolute values
|\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}|
and
|\mathbf{Y}_{n}|
. This can be achieved by considering
m
more loss functions
\ell^{(r+m)}=-\ell^{(r)}
for
r\in[m]
and applying
Theorem

4.13
to these
2m
loss functions.

Proof of
Theorem

4.13
.

The proof is based on a more refined version of the asymptotic linear representation (
34
) using the sub-Weibull stability condition.
Let

\bm{\zeta}_{n}=\sqrt{n}S_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{-1}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}-\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K})-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}S_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\bm{\ell}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})\,.

By
Lemma

4.20
, each
\zeta_{n,r}
, the
r
th coordinate of
\bm{\zeta}_{n}
, is
(2\epsilon_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}},\alpha+1)
-SW.

Consider i.i.d. random vectors
\mathbf{Z}_{i}=S_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{-1}\bar{\bm{\ell}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})
. Condition (
55
)
and
Lemma

2.12
imply that
\max|\mathbf{Z}_{1}|
is
(c_{\alpha}(\log m)^{\alpha}\kappa,\alpha)
-SW and hence

\|\max|\mathbf{Z}_{1}|\|_{4}\leq c_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\log m)^{\alpha}\kappa
for another constant
c_{\alpha}^{\prime}
. On the other hand, by (
55
)
\|\bar{\ell}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{(r)}/\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{(r)}\|_{4}\leq 4^{\alpha}\kappa
for each
r\in[m]
.
Now let
\mathbf{W}_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}S_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\bm{\ell}}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(X_{i})
.
Theorem

4.12
implies that, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}

|\mathbb{P}(\max\mathbf{W}_{n}\leq t)-\mathbb{P}(\max\mathbf{Y}_{n}\leq t)|\leq C_{\kappa,\alpha}\frac{(\log m)^{\alpha+5/4}}{n^{1/4}}=o(1)\,,

(58)

where the last
o(1)
statement comes from the first part of (
56
).

Under the second part of (
56
), one can find a
\delta_{n}
such that
\delta_{n}\sqrt{\log m}=o(1)
and
\epsilon_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(\log m)^{\alpha+1}=o(\delta_{n})
(for example, one possibility is
\delta_{n}=\epsilon_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{1/2}(\log m)^{\alpha/2}
).

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\max(\mathbf{W}_{n}+\bm{\zeta}_{n})\leq t\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\max\mathbf{W}_{n}\leq t+\delta_{n}\right]+\mathbb{P}(\max|\bm{\zeta}_{n}|>\delta_{n})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\max\mathbf{Y}_{n}\leq t+\delta_{n})+o(1)+o(1)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\max\mathbf{Y}_{n}\leq t)+C\delta_{n}\sqrt{\log m}+o(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\max\mathbf{Y}_{n}\leq t)+o(1)\,,

where the first
o(1)
in the second inequality comes from (
58
), the second
o(1)
in the same line comes from the fact that
\max|\bm{\zeta}_{n}|
is
(c_{\alpha}\epsilon_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(\log m)^{\alpha+1},\alpha+1)
-SW as guaranteed by
Lemma

2.12
and the choice of
\delta_{n}
, and the last inequality comes from a Gaussian anti-concentration result
(
Chernozhukov et al. 2022
, Lemma J.3)
.
Similarly we can obtain the other direction and conclude the proof.
∎

Remark 4.15

(Gaussian Comparison with Deterministic Centering)
.

It is possible to extend
Theorem

4.13
to deterministic centering. The result will be analogous to
Proposition

4.11
. When
\nabla_{1}R^{(r)}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})
is
(n^{-1}\sigma_{n}^{(r)}\epsilon_{n},\alpha)
-SW for all
r\in[m]
with
\epsilon_{n}
vanishing fast enough in the sense of (
56
), then
Theorem

4.13
would hold for the deterministic centering without much modification. Otherwise, when
\nabla_{1}R^{(r)}
is large as in the second case in
Proposition

4.11
, the scaling would be
\tau_{n}^{(r)}
, and the corresponding Gaussian vector would have correlation matrix whose
(r,s)
-entry equals
{\rm Corr}(\bar{\ell}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{(r)}(X_{0})+\bar{g}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{(r)}(X_{0}),~\bar{\ell}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{(s)}(X_{0})+\bar{g}_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{(s)}(X_{0}))
. See
Section

4.5
for further details. Practically, the random centering Gaussian comparison is easier to use with easier-to-verify assumptions. We provide a a couple of inference examples using random centering Gaussian comparison in
Section

5
.

Variance and Correlation Estimation

In order to use the high-dimensional Gaussian comparison
Theorem

4.13
to construct confidence sets for
\bar{\mathbf{R}}_{{\mathrm{cv}},n,K}
, one needs to estimate not only the marginal variances
(\sigma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}^{(r)})^{2}
for
r\in[m]
, but also the pairwise correlation
\Gamma_{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}(r,s)
in (
53
). The same argument in the proof of
Theorem

4.6
can provide entry-wise
L_{2}
-consistency of the sample variance and correlation estimates. However, such entry-wise consistency is not sufficient for high-dimensional inference, because when
m=m_{n}
increases with
n
, the Gaussian comparison result is not a central limit theorem as there is no fixed limit of convergence in distribution. To make valid inference with the estimated variances and correlations, we need a more refined error analysis of the estimated variance and covariance, which will be detailed in
Section

5.1
below.

Rates of Convergence

In this article we do not keep track of the rates of convergence in CLT or Gaussian comparison as we focus on asymptotically valid inference which only requires vanishing Kolmogorov-Smirnov distance between the scaled and centered CV risk estimate and the corresponding Gaussian distribution. As mentioned in
Remark

4.4
, such rates of convergence are available by adapting the proofs, assuming a specific rate of convergence on the loss stability.

4.4
Auxiliary Lemmas

In this subsection we collect the auxiliary lemmas used in earlier subsections.

The following lemma explains the “free nabla” trick.

Lemma 4.16

(Free Nabla Lemma)
.

Let
\mathbf{D}=(X_{1},...,X_{n},X_{1}^{\prime},...,X_{n}^{\prime},W)
be independent random objects such that for each
i\in[n]
,
X_{i}
and
X_{i}^{\prime}
have the same distribution.
Let
h
and
g
be two functions that act on subsets
\mathbf{D}
such that for an
i\in[n]

1.

g(\mathbf{D})
,
h(\mathbf{D})
, and
g(\mathbf{D})h(\mathbf{D})
all have finite
L_{1}
norm,

2.

\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}g=0
, and

3.

h
does not involve
X_{i}^{\prime}
.

Then

\mathbb{E}(gh)=\mathbb{E}(g\nabla_{i}h)\,.

The operator
\nabla_{i}
in this lemma is defined in the usual sense since
h
does not involve
X_{i}^{\prime}
. In particular,
\nabla_{i}h
is the difference between the original
h
and the one obtained
by replacing
X_{i}
by
X_{i}^{\prime}
in the calculation of
h
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.16
.

Let
h^{i}
be the version of
h
obtained by swapping
X_{i}
and
X_{i}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{D}
. It suffices to show that
\mathbb{E}(gh^{i})=0
.
In fact, by construction
h^{i}
does not involve
X_{i}
since
h
does not involve
X_{i}^{\prime}
. Thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}gh^{i}=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}gh^{i})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}g)h^{i}\right]=0\,.\qed

The following lemma provides a convenient bound on
\nabla_{i}\xi_{j}
for
i\notin I_{k_{j}}
.

Lemma 4.17
.

For
i\neq j\in[n]
we have

\|\nabla_{i}\xi_{j}\|_{2}\leq\|\nabla_{i}\ell(X_{j},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{j}})\|_{2}
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.17
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{j}}^{i}
be the dataset obtained by replacing
X_{i}
by
X_{i}^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{j}}
while keeping everything else the same.
Then

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{i}\xi_{j}\|_{2}^{2}=

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{i}\ell(X_{j},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{j}})\|_{2}^{2}-2\mathbb{E}\nabla_{i}\ell(X_{j},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{j}})\nabla_{i}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{j}})+\|\nabla_{i}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{j}})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{i}\ell(X_{j},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{j}})\|_{2}^{2}-\|\nabla_{i}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{j}})\|_{2}^{2}\,,

where the last step follows from

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\nabla_{i}\ell(X_{j},{\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{j}})\nabla_{i}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{j}})=\|\nabla_{i}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n,-k_{j}})\|_{2}^{2}

by first taking expectation over
X_{j}
.
∎

The following lemma is a special case of Rio’s inequality
Theorem

2.13
and generalizes
Theorem

2.14
.

Lemma 4.18

(Rio’s Inequality For Conditionally Independent Sequence)
.

Let
Z_{1},...,Z_{n}
be random variables that are conditionally independent given a
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathbb{E}(Z_{i}|\mathcal{C})=0
for all
i
. Then for any
q\geq 2
,

\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\right\|_{q}^{2}\leq(q-1)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|Z_{i}\|_{q}^{2}\,.

Moreover, if
Z_{i}
is
(\kappa_{i},\alpha_{i})
-SW (
i=1,...,n
) then

\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}
is
(\kappa,\alpha)
-SW with

\kappa=2^{\alpha}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{i}^{2}}
and
\alpha=\max_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}+1/2
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.18
.

The proof is identical to that of
Theorem

2.14
.
∎

Lemma

4.18
can be used to prove various important consequences of the loss stability, collected in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.19

(Useful Consequences of Loss Stability)
.

Assume an i.i.d. data sequence and a symmetric estimator. Let
