we make this more rigorous and general for the stochastic gradient descent algorithm. In
Section

6.3
we illustrate the subtlety of the stability of the difference loss functions (see
Section

5.1
for the context) in a prototypical nonparametric regression example, where the stability is achieved by another form of regularization: truncated basis expansion. In
Section

6.4
we consider the stability conditions involved in the consistency analysis of online rolling validation in
Section

3.3
, focusing on a combination of stochastic gradient descent and nonparametric basis expansion, known as the sieve SGD (introduced in (
24
) in
Section

3.2
).

6.1
Stability of Stochastic Gradient Descent

Consider the M-estimation problem

\hat{f}=\arg\min_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi(f;X_{i})

for some objective function
\varphi:\mathcal{F}\times{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{1}
, and the stochastic gradient descent algorithm (SGD):

1.

Initialize
\hat{f}_{0}=0

2.

For
t=0,...,n-1
, update

\hat{f}_{t+1}=\hat{f}_{t}-\alpha_{t+1}\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{t};X_{t+1})\,,

(\alpha_{t}:1\leq t\leq n)
is a sequence of learning rates, and
\dot{\varphi}(\cdot;x)
is the gradient of
\varphi(\cdot;x)
viewed as a function of
f
.

3.

The final estimate
\hat{f}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})=\hat{f}_{n}
.

One may also consider the averaged final estimate
\hat{f}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})=n^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\hat{f}_{t}
. The stability results derived below can be carried over to the averaged case. The SGD estimate may appear to violate the symmetry condition in
Definition

2.1
. This issue can be resolved by randomizing the order of sample points in the SGD update sequence.

The motivation of analyzing (approximate) M-estimators obtained by SGD is quite natural. First, M-estimators cover a wide range of parametric and nonparametric estimators such as the maximum likelihood estimator, the least squares estimator, local polynomial regression, and their robust counterparts; and machine learning tools such as kernel regression, support vector machine, and neural networks. Second, the SGD algorithm is arguably the most popular method for large-scale M-estimation. Under convexity and smoothness conditions on the objective function
\varphi
, the simple form of SGD allows precise characterization of the algorithmic behavior of the estimated parameter and hence facilitates a general and abstract stability analysis.

As seen in the ridge regression, the strong convexity of the objective function
\varphi(\cdot,x)
plays an important role in the stability of the resulting estimator.

Definition 6.1

(Strong convexity)
.

Let
\gamma
be a positive number. A function
h:\mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is
\gamma
-strongly convex if

h(u+v)-h(u)\geq\langle\dot{h}(u),~v\rangle+\frac{\gamma}{2}\|v\|^{2}
, where
\|\cdot\|
denotes the Euclidean or Hilbert space norm.

Assumption 6.2

(Smoothness and convexity of objective function)
.

There exists positive constants
(\gamma,C_{0},\beta)
such that for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}

(a)

\varphi(\cdot;x)
is
\gamma
-strongly convex.

(b)

\varphi(\cdot;x)
is
C_{0}
-Lipschitz

(c)

\dot{\varphi}(\cdot;x)
is
\beta
-Lipschitz

Part (c) of
6.2
is also known as “
\beta
-smoothness”. These are among the standard assumptions in the analysis of the convergence and stability of SGD. In deep neural networks, it is unrealistic to assume convexity of
\varphi(\cdot;x)
, but we can assume that these conditions hold in a local convergence basin. Parts (a) and (c) require that
\gamma\leq\beta
. When
\gamma=\beta
,
\varphi(\cdot;x)
is to a quadratic function. The gap between
\gamma/\beta
and
1
reflects how close
\varphi(\cdot;x)
is a quadratic function. So the conditions are more stringent if we require a large value of
\gamma/\beta
.

Example 6.3

(Ridge Regression)
.

Consider
x=(y,z)
and
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\mathbb{B}(r_{x})
,
\mathcal{F}=\mathbb{B}(r_{f})
(
\mathbb{B}(r)
denotes an centered ball with radius
r
.)

\varphi(f;x)=\frac{1}{2}(y-z^{T}f)^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\|f\|^{2}\,.

Then
\varphi(\cdot;x)
satisfies
6.2
with
\gamma=\lambda
,
C_{0}=r_{x}^{2}(1+r_{f})+\lambda r_{f}
, and
\beta=r_{x}^{2}+\lambda
.

In practice, the objective function
\varphi(\cdot,;x)
usually is not strongly convex without a regularizing penalty term such as the ridge penalty. It is still possible to establish similar stability results for SGD if
\mathbb{E}_{X}\varphi(\cdot;X)
is strongly convex. We provide a detailed example in
Section

6.4
for the sieve SGD estimator introduced in
Section

3.2
.

The main consequence of
6.2
is the following contractivity result of SGD.

Proposition 6.4

(Contractivity of SGD)
.

Assume
\varphi
satisfies parts (a), (c) of
6.2
, and
\alpha\in(0,2/(\beta+\gamma))
.
The SGD update mapping
G_{\varphi,\alpha,x}:\mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathcal{F}

G_{\varphi,\alpha,x}(f)=f-\alpha\dot{\varphi}(f;x)

satisfies

\|G_{\varphi,\alpha,x}(u)-G_{\varphi,\alpha,x}(v)\|\leq\left(1-\frac{\beta\gamma}{\beta+\gamma}\alpha\right)\|u-v\|\,.

The proof of
Proposition

6.4
, though elementary, is non-trivial. We will not provide a proof here. See the bibliographic notes in
Section

6.5
for further references. However, a one-line proof can be obtained, with a different contraction factor, if we assume that
\varphi(\cdot,x)
is twice differentiable: Let
\ddot{\varphi}(\cdot,x)
be the Hessian matrix, which is lower bounded by
\gamma I
because of strong convexity. Then, by the middle value theorem we have

G_{\varphi,\alpha,x}(u)-G_{\varphi,\alpha,x}(v)=(I-\alpha\ddot{\varphi}(w,x))(u-v)

for some
w\in[u,v]
, where
[u,v]
denotes the line segment with endpoints
(u,v)
.

Theorem 6.5

(First-order stability of SGD)
.

Under
6.2
, if the learning rate satisfies
\alpha_{t}=\beta^{-1}t^{-a}
for an
a\in(0,1)
and

\frac{\gamma}{\beta+\gamma}\geq\frac{a(1-a)}{1-2^{-(1-a)}}\frac{\log n}{n^{1-a}}\,,

then for any
i\in[n]
we have

\left\|\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{n}\right\|\leq\frac{2^{1+a}C_{0}}{\beta}n^{-a}\,.

The intuition behind
Theorem

6.5
is straightforward.
Assume sample point
X_{i}
is perturbed to
X_{i}^{\prime}
. The SGD algorithm will remain the same up to time
i
, and the future updates will be applied to the perturbed solution at time
i
. If
i
is small, then
X_{i}
appears early in the SGD iteration, the resulting perturbation on
\hat{f}_{i}
will be shrunk exponentially in the remaining iterations due to the regularity conditions in
6.2
. If
i
is large, the learning rate is already very small which upper bounds the perturbation incurred by changing
X_{i}
to
X_{i}^{\prime}
.

As mentioned in the intuition above, the proof of
Theorem

6.5

is a direct consequence of the following lemma, which establishes the multiple-step contractivity of SGD updates under
6.2
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Under
6.2
and the conditions in
Theorem

6.5
, we have for all
t\geq i

\left\|\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{t}\right\|\leq\frac{2C_{0}}{\beta}i^{-a}\exp\left[-\frac{\gamma}{(1-a)(\beta+\gamma)}\left\{(t+1)^{1-a}-(i+1)^{1-a}\right\}\right]\,.

Proof of
Lemma

6.6
.

By construction,

\|\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{i}\|=\alpha_{i}\|\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{i-1};X_{i})-\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{i-1};X_{i}^{\prime})\|\leq 2C_{0}\alpha_{i}=\frac{2C_{0}}{\beta}i^{-a}
.

By contractivity of SGD (
Proposition

6.4
), for all
t>i
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{t}\|\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2C_{0}}{\beta}i^{-a}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}\left(1-\frac{\beta\gamma}{\beta+\gamma}\alpha_{j}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2C_{0}}{\beta}i^{-a}\exp\left(-\frac{\beta\gamma}{\beta+\gamma}\sum_{j=i+1}^{t}\alpha_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2C_{0}}{\beta}i^{-a}\exp\left(-\frac{\gamma}{\beta+\gamma}\sum_{j=i+1}^{t}j^{-a}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2C_{0}}{\beta}i^{-a}\exp\left(-\frac{\gamma}{\beta+\gamma}\int_{x=i+1}^{t+1}x^{-a}dx\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2C_{0}}{\beta}i^{-a}\exp\left[-\frac{\gamma}{(1-a)(\beta+\gamma)}\left\{(t+1)^{1-a}-(i+1)^{1-a}\right\}\right]\,.\qed

Proof of
Theorem

6.5
.

If
i>n/2
,

\|\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{n}\|\leq\|\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{i}\|\leq\frac{(n/2)^{-a}}{\beta}2C_{0}\,.

If
i\leq n/2
, according to
Lemma

6.6
with
t=n
we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{n}\|\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2C_{0}}{\beta}\exp\left[-\frac{\gamma}{(1-a)(\beta+\gamma)}\left\{(n+1)^{1-a}-(i+1)^{1-a}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2C_{0}}{\beta}\exp\left[-\frac{\gamma}{(1-a)(\beta+\gamma)}(n+1)^{1-a}(1-2^{-(1-a)})\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2C_{0}}{\beta}\exp(-a\log n)\,.\qed

6.2
Second Order Stability

The second order stability concerns the quantity
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
for
i\neq j\in[n]
, and is used in the deterministic centering CLT in
Section

4.2
. Although the discussion in
Section

4.2
emphasizes that it is more important to compare
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})
with
\nabla_{i}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})
. An upper bound of
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\ell(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
would still be useful as it provides an upper bound of
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})
.
Now we control
\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\hat{f}
, which, by construction of SGD, is equal to
\nabla_{j}\nabla_{i}\hat{f}
.

We first show how to control the second order loss stability through the second order parameter stability.

Proposition 6.7
.

If the loss function
\ell(x,f):{\mathcal{X}}\times\mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathbb{R}

is
c_{0}
-Lipschitz,
\dot{\ell}(x,\cdot)
is
c_{1}
-Lipschitz for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
then

|\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\ell(x,\hat{f}_{n})|\leq c_{0}\|\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\hat{f}_{n}\|+2c_{1}\epsilon_{n,i,j}^{2}\,,

where
\epsilon_{n,i,j}=\max\{\|\nabla_{j}\hat{f}_{n}\|\,,~\|\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{n}^{j}\|\,,~\|\nabla_{j}\hat{f}_{n}^{i}\|\}
, and

\hat{f}_{n}^{i}
(
\hat{f}_{n}^{j}
) denotes the estimate obtained with
X_{i}
(
X_{j}
) replaced by its i.i.d. copy.

One example of such
\epsilon_{n,i,j}
is given in
Lemma

6.6
, which implies that
\epsilon_{n,i,j}^{2}
has order
n^{-2a}
. The main result in this subsection is to show that
\|\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\hat{f}_{n}\|
can also be bounded by
n^{-2a}
up to a logarithm factor.

Proof of
Proposition

6.7
.

Let
\hat{f}_{n}^{ij}
be the estimate obtained with
(X_{i},X_{j})
replaced by their i.i.d. copies.
By middle value theorem, there exists
\tilde{f}\in[\hat{f}_{n},~\hat{f}_{n}^{i}]
and
\tilde{f}^{\prime}\in[\hat{f}_{n}^{j},~\hat{f}_{n}^{ij}]
such that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\ell(x,\hat{f}_{n})|=

\displaystyle\left|\langle\hat{f}_{n}-\hat{f}_{n}^{i}\,,~\dot{\ell}(x,\tilde{f})\rangle-\langle\hat{f}_{n}^{j}-\hat{f}_{n}^{ij}\,,~\dot{\ell}(x,\tilde{f}^{\prime})\rangle\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\langle\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\hat{f}_{n}\,,~\dot{\ell}(x,\tilde{f})\rangle+\langle\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{n}^{j}\,,~\dot{\ell}(x,\tilde{f})-\dot{\ell}(x,\tilde{f}^{\prime})\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c_{0}\|\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\hat{f}_{n}\|+c_{1}\|\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{n}^{j}\|\cdot\|\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}^{\prime}\|\,.

The claimed result follows from the fact that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}^{\prime}\|\leq

\displaystyle\max\left\{\|\hat{f}_{n}-\hat{f}_{n}^{j}\|\,,~\|\hat{f}_{n}-\hat{f}_{n}^{ij}\|\,,~\|\hat{f}_{n}^{i}-\hat{f}_{n}^{j}\|\,,\|\hat{f}_{n}^{i}-\hat{f}_{n}^{ij}\|\right\}\leq 2\epsilon_{n,i,j}\,.\qed

Our main additional condition for the second-order stability of SGD estimates is the smoothness of Hessian of
\varphi(\cdot;x)
.

Assumption 6.8
.

The Hessian matrix of
\varphi(\cdot;x)
, denoted
\ddot{\varphi}(\cdot;x)
, is
C_{2}
-Lipschitz for a constant
C_{2}
and all
x
:

\|\ddot{\varphi}(f;x)-\ddot{\varphi}(f^{\prime};x)\|_{\rm op}\leq C_{2}\|f-f^{\prime}\|

for all
f\,,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\rm op}
denotes the operator norm.

It may be possible to avoid such a smooth Hessian condition using a similar co-coerciveness argument as in the proof of
Proposition

6.4
. Here we simply work under
6.8
for simplicity and transparency of the proof.

Example 6.9
.

Ridge logistic regression:
x=(y,z)\in\mathbb{B}(r_{x})
, and
f\in\mathbb{B}(r_{f})
.

\varphi(f;x)=-y(z^{T}f)+\log(1+e^{z^{T}f})+\lambda\|f\|_{2}\,.

Then
\varphi
satisfies
6.2
and
6.8
with
\gamma=2\lambda
,
C_{0}=r_{x}^{2}+r_{f}^{-1}\log(1+e^{r_{x}r_{f}})+\lambda r_{f}
,
\beta=r_{x}/r_{f}+\lambda
, and
C_{2}=r_{x}^{2}/(4r_{f})
.

Theorem 6.10

(Second-Order Stability of SGD)
.

Under the same assumptions as in
Theorem

6.5
, assume in addition that
\varphi
satisfies
6.8
such that
\alpha_{t}\gamma\leq 1
for all
t
, then

\|\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\hat{f}\|\leq cn^{-2a}\log n\,,

for a constant
c
depending on
(a,\beta,\gamma,C_{0},C_{2})

Proof.

Assume
i<j
. Let
\hat{f}_{t}^{i}
be the estimate at time
t
with
X_{i}^{\prime}
as the
i
th sample point. Define
\hat{f}_{t}^{ij}
correspondingly.

Then

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\hat{f}_{j}\right\|=

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{f}_{j}-\hat{f}^{i}_{j}-\hat{f}^{j}_{j}+\hat{f}^{ij}_{j}\right\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\|\hat{f}_{j-1}-\alpha_{j}\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{j-1};X_{j})-\hat{f}^{i}_{j-1}+\alpha_{j}\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}^{i}_{j-1};X_{j})

\displaystyle-\hat{f}_{j-1}+\alpha_{j}\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{j-1};X_{j}^{\prime})+\hat{f}^{i}_{j-1}-\alpha_{j}\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}^{i}_{j-1};X_{j}^{\prime})\big\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{j}\left\|\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{j-1};X_{j})-\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}^{i}_{j-1};X_{j})-\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{j-1};X_{j}^{\prime})+\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}^{i}_{j-1};X_{j}^{\prime})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\beta\alpha_{j}\|\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{j-1}\|\,,

(86)

where the last inequality follows from the Lipschitz property of
\dot{\varphi}(\cdot;x)
.

For
t>j
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\hat{f}_{t}=

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{t}-\hat{f}^{i}_{t}-\hat{f}^{j}_{t}+\hat{f}^{ij}_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{t-1}-\alpha_{t}\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{t};X_{t})-\hat{f}^{i}_{t-1}+\alpha_{t}\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}^{i}_{t-1};X_{t})

\displaystyle-\hat{f}^{j}_{t-1}+\alpha_{t}\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}^{j}_{t-1};X_{t})+\hat{f}^{ij}_{t-1}-\alpha_{t}\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}^{ij}_{t-1};X_{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\hat{f}_{t-1}-\alpha_{t}\left[\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{t-1};X_{t})-\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{t-1}^{i};X_{t})-\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j};X_{t})+\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{t-1}^{ij};X_{t})\right]\,.

(87)

Using the middle value theorem, there exist
\tilde{f}_{t-1}\in[\hat{f}_{t-1},\hat{f}_{t-1}^{i}]
and
\tilde{f}_{t-1}^{\prime}\in[\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j},\hat{f}_{t-1}^{ij}]
such that

\displaystyle\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{t-1};X_{t})-\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{t-1}^{i};X_{t})-\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j};X_{t})+\dot{\varphi}(\hat{f}_{t-1}^{ij};X_{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ddot{\varphi}(\tilde{f}_{t-1};X_{t})(\hat{f}_{t-1}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{i})-\ddot{\varphi}(\tilde{f}_{t-1}^{\prime};X_{t})(\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{ij})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ddot{\varphi}(\tilde{f}_{t-1};X_{t})\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\hat{f}_{t-1}+\left[\ddot{\varphi}(\tilde{f}_{t-1};X_{t})-\ddot{\varphi}(\tilde{f}_{t-1}^{\prime};X_{t})\right](\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{ij})\,.

Plugging this to the RHS of (
87
), we get

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\hat{f}_{t}=\left[I-\alpha_{t}\ddot{\varphi}(\tilde{f}_{t-1};X_{t})\right]\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\hat{f}_{t-1}+\alpha_{t}\left[\ddot{\varphi}(\tilde{f}_{t-1};X_{t})-\ddot{\varphi}(\tilde{f}_{t-1}^{\prime};X_{t})\right](\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{ij})\,.

Let
\eta_{t}=\|\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\hat{f}_{t}\|
, we get

\eta_{t}\leq(1-\alpha_{t}\gamma)\eta_{t-1}+2C_{2}\alpha_{t}\|\tilde{f}_{t-1}-\tilde{f}_{t-1}^{\prime}\|\cdot\|\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{ij}\|\,,

(88)

where the second term on the RHS uses Lipschitz property of
\ddot{\varphi}
(with Lipschitz constant
C_{2}
).

To control
\tilde{f}_{t-1}-\tilde{f}_{t-1}^{\prime}
, apply
Lemma

6.6
directly to
\hat{f}_{t-1}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j}
and
\hat{f}_{t-1}^{i}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{ij}
, and then use triangle inequality on
\hat{f}_{t-1}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{ij}=(\hat{f}_{t-1}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j})+(\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{ij})
and
\hat{f}^{i}_{t-1}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j}=(\hat{f}_{t-1}^{i}-\hat{f}_{t-1})+(\hat{f}_{t-1}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j})
, then we have,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{f}_{t-1}-\tilde{f}_{t-1}^{\prime}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max\left\{\|\hat{f}_{t-1}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j}\|\,,~\|\hat{f}_{t-1}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{ij}\|\,,~\|\hat{f}_{t-1}^{i}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{j}\|\,,~\|\hat{f}_{t-1}^{i}-\hat{f}_{t-1}^{ij}\|\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2C_{0}}{\beta}\left(\delta_{t-1,i}(c)i^{-a}+\delta_{t-1,j}(c)j^{-a}\right)\,,

where
c=\frac{\gamma}{(1-a)(\beta+\gamma)}
and

\delta_{t,j}(c)=\exp\left[-c((t+1)^{1-a}-(j+1)^{1-a})\right]\,.

For
t\geq j+1
, define

\displaystyle\varrho_{i,j,t}=

\displaystyle\frac{8C_{2}C_{0}^{2}}{\beta^{3}}t^{-a}\left[\delta_{t-1,i}(c)i^{-a}+\delta_{t-1,j}(c)j^{-a}\right]\delta_{t-1,i}(c)i^{-a}\,,

then (
88
) implies, for
t\geq j+1
,

\eta_{t}\leq(1-\alpha_{t}\gamma)\eta_{t-1}+\varrho_{i,j,t}\,.

(89)

We now seek to upper-bound
\eta_{n}
using the recursion (
88
). To do so, let
c^{\prime}=\gamma/[(1-a)\beta]\geq c
. Thus

\displaystyle\eta_{n}\leq

\displaystyle(1-\alpha_{n}\gamma)\eta_{n-1}+\varrho_{i,j,n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1-\alpha_{n}\gamma)\left[(1-\alpha_{n-1}\gamma)\eta_{n-2}+\varrho_{i,j,n-1}\right]+\varrho_{i,j,n}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left[\prod_{k=j+1}^{n}(1-\alpha_{j}\gamma)\right]\eta_{j}+\sum_{k=j+1}^{n}\left[\prod_{l=k+1}^{n}(1-\alpha_{l}\gamma)\right]\varrho_{i,j,k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\delta_{n,j}(c^{\prime})\eta_{j}+\sum_{k=j+1}^{n}\delta_{n,k}(c^{\prime})\varrho_{i,j,k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\delta_{n,j}(c^{\prime})\eta_{j}}_{(III)}+\underbrace{\frac{8C_{2}C_{0}^{2}}{\beta^{3}}\sum_{k=j+1}^{n}\delta_{n,k}(c^{\prime})\delta_{k-1,i}^{2}(c)k^{-a}i^{-2a}}_{(II)}

\displaystyle~~~+\underbrace{\frac{8C_{2}C_{0}^{2}}{\beta^{3}}\sum_{k=j+1}^{n}\delta_{n,k}(c^{\prime})\delta_{k-1,i}(c)\delta_{k-1,j}(c)k^{-a}i^{-a}j^{-a}}_{(I)}\,,

(90)

where the last inequality appeals to the fact that for positive integers
j<t
,
\prod_{j+1}^{t}(1-\alpha_{j}\gamma)\leq\exp\left[-\frac{\gamma}{(1-a)\beta}\left((t+1)^{1-a}-(j+1)^{1-a}\right)\right]
.

Term (I):
Using the fact that
\delta_{k-1,i}(c)\geq\delta_{k-1,i}(c^{\prime})
,
\delta_{n,k}(c)\delta_{k-1,i}(c)\leq\delta_{n,i}(c)e^{c(2^{1-a}-1)}
, and
\delta_{k-1,j}(c)\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle(I)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{8C_{2}C_{0}^{2}}{\beta^{3}}e^{c(2^{1-a}-1)}i^{-a}j^{-a}\delta_{n,i}(c)\sum_{k=j+1}^{n}k^{-a}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{8C_{2}C_{0}^{2}}{(1-a)\beta^{3}}e^{c(2^{1-a}-1)}i^{-a}j^{-a}\delta_{n,i}(c)(n^{1-a}-j^{1-a})\,.

(91)

Let
c_{2}=(3-a)/[c(1-a)]
.
When
i\leq n-c_{2}(n+1)^{a}\log n
, we have

\displaystyle(n+1)^{1-a}-(i+1)^{1-a}=

\displaystyle(n+1)^{1-a}\left[1-\left(1-\frac{n-i}{n+1}\right)^{1-a}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(n+1)^{1-a}(1-a)\frac{n-i}{n+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{2}(1-a)\log n\,,

where the second lines uses
(1-x)^{b}\leq 1-bx
for
x\in[0,1)
and
b\in(0,1)
. Thus
(
91
) implies

\displaystyle(I)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{8C_{2}C_{0}^{2}}{(1-a)\beta^{3}}e^{c(2^{1-a}-1)}i^{-a}j^{-a}n^{-(3-a)}(n^{1-a}-j^{1-a})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{8C_{2}C_{0}^{2}}{(1-a)\beta^{3}}e^{c(2^{1-a}-1)}n^{-2}\,.

(92)

When
i\geq n-c_{2}(n+1)^{a}\log n
. Suppose
n
is large enough that
n-c_{2}(n+1)^{a}\log n\geq n/2
. Use the following upper bounds in the RHS of (
91
):

k^{-a}\leq j^{-a}\leq i^{-a}\leq 2^{a}n^{-a}
,
\delta_{n,i}(c)\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle(I)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{8C_{2}C_{0}^{2}}{(1-a)\beta^{3}}e^{c(2^{1-a}-1)}2^{3a}n^{-3a}(n-j)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{8C_{2}C_{0}^{2}}{(1-a)\beta^{3}}e^{c(2^{1-a}-1)}2^{3a}n^{-3a}(n-i)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{8C_{2}C_{0}^{2}}{(1-a)\beta^{3}}e^{c(2^{1-a}-1)}2^{3a}n^{-3a}c_{2}(n+1)^{a}\log n

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{8C_{2}C_{0}^{2}}{(1-a)\beta^{3}}e^{c(2^{1-a}-1)}2^{3a+1}c_{2}n^{-2a}\log n\,.

(93)

Combining (
92
) and (
93
), we conclude that

(I)\leq c(a,\beta,\gamma,C_{0},C_{2})n^{-2a}\log n\,,

(94)

for
n
large enough.

Term (II):

The second term in (
90
) is upper bounded by, for large enough
n
,

\displaystyle(II)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{8C_{2}C_{0}^{2}}{\beta^{3}}i^{-2a}\sum_{k=j+1}^{n}k^{-a}\delta_{n,k}(c)\delta_{k-1,i}(c)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{8C_{0}^{2}C_{2}}{(1-a)\beta^{3}}e^{c(2^{1-a}-1)}i^{-2a}\delta_{n,i}(c)(n^{1-a}-j^{1-a})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c(a,\beta,\gamma,C_{0},C_{2})n^{-2a}\log n\,,

(95)

where the first inequality follows from
\delta_{k-1,i}(c)\leq 1
, the second and third follow from the same argument as in term (I).

Term (III):

\displaystyle(III)=

\displaystyle\delta_{n,j}(c^{\prime})\eta_{j}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\beta\alpha_{j}\|\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{j-1}\|\delta_{n,j}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{4C_{0}}{\beta}j^{-a}i^{-a}\delta_{j-1,i}(c^{\prime})\delta_{n,j}(c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{4C_{0}}{\beta}e^{c(2^{1-a}-1)}j^{-a}i^{-a}\delta_{n,i}(c)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{4C_{0}}{\beta}e^{c(2^{1-a}-1)}i^{-2a}\delta_{n,i}(c)\,,

(96)

where the first inequality follows from (
86
), the second from
Lemma

6.6
, the third from
\delta_{j-1,i}(c)\geq\delta_{j-1,i}(c^{\prime})
and a same argument used in the treatment of term (I), and the fourth one from the fact that
j>i
.

Finally, a similar argument of dichotomizing
i
according to
i\leq n/2
and
i\geq n/2
as in the proof of
Theorem

6.5
leads to the conclusion

(III)\leq c(a,\beta,\gamma,C_{0},C_{2})n^{-2a}\,.

(97)

The proof concludes by plugging (
94
), (
95
), and (
97
) into (
90
).
∎

6.3
Difference Loss Stability

According to
Remark

5.5
, a technical condition needed for the validity of the difference-based CV model confidence set involves stability conditions on the standardized loss differences, which is used to establish high-dimensional Gaussian comparison for the cross-validated risk differences. This often becomes tricky because the loss differences usually have vanishing variances, and standardization means dividing the difference loss function by a vanishing standard deviation, which could make the stability condition less likely to hold. However, a more optimistic argument is that this condition retains its original intuition: taking a
\nabla
operator reduces the magnitude of the difference loss by more than a factor of
1/\sqrt{n}
, even though the difference loss itself may be vanishing. In this subsection we will illustrate this intuition through a simplified nonparametric regression problem using truncated basis expansion estimates.

In this subsection we will not keep track of constant factors, so we use the symbol “
\lesssim
” to denote “upper bound up to a constant factor” and “
a\asymp b
” for “
a\lesssim b
and
b\lesssim a
”.

Let
(X_{i}:1\leq i\leq n)
be i.i.d. vectors with
X_{i}=(Y_{i},Z_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, such that

Y_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}f_{j}Z_{ij}+\epsilon_{i}

(98)

satisfying the following assumptions.

Assumption 6.11
.

1.

\epsilon_{i}
is independent of
Z_{i}
, has mean zero, finite variance
\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}
, and are uniformly bounded by a constant;

2.

Z_{ij}
is mean zero, has unit variance, uniformly bounded by a constant, and are mutually independent;

3.

The regression coefficients
f_{j}\asymp j^{-\frac{1+a}{2}}
for some
a>0
for all
j\geq 1
.

The conditions in
6.11
are stronger than technically necessary, and are made for a simpler presentation.
The uniform bound on
\epsilon
and
Z
can be relaxed to sub-Weibull tail conditions, and the independence between the entries of
Z
can be relaxed to
\mathbb{E}(Z_{ij}|Z_{i1},...,Z_{i,j-1})=0
for all
j
. The independence between
\epsilon
and
Z
can be relaxed in a similar fashion.
The assumed exact rate of
f_{j}
in Part 3 is more stringent than the usual Sobolov ellipsoid condition which only assumes upper bound on
|f_{j}|
. We need the lower bound on
|f_{j}|
to track the bias and variance precisely. More discussion can be found in
Section

6.5
.

For each
j
, we can naturally estimate
f_{j}
by

\hat{f}_{j}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}Z_{ij}\,.

We consider a truncated estimate of
f=(f_{j}:j\geq 1)
. Let
J
be a positive integer,

\hat{f}^{(J)}=(\hat{f}_{j}^{(J)}:j\geq 1)

where

\hat{f}_{j}^{(J)}\coloneqq\hat{f}_{j}\mathds{1}(j\leq J)\,,~~\forall~j\geq 1\,.

(99)

The tuning parameter
J
controls the number of basis used in the truncated estimation.

The optimal choice of
J
depends on the sample size.
For each candidate fitting procedure
r
, let
J_{r}
be a positive integer varying with the sample size
n
such that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}J_{r}=\infty
at a speed specified by the estimator
r
. For example
J_{r}=n^{\tau_{r}}
, where
\{\tau_{r}:r\in\mathcal{[}m]\}
is a collection of positive numbers corresponding to different growth rates of
J_{r}
as the sample size
n
increases.
We will use
\hat{f}^{(r)}
to denote
\hat{f}^{(J_{r})}
for convenience and the estimators will be evaluated under the squared loss:
\ell((y,z),f)=(y-f(z))^{2}
.

Now we are ready to quantify the stability conditions for the difference loss
\ell^{(r,s)}(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
and the difference risk
R^{(r,s)}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})
.

Proposition 6.12

(Difference Loss Stability)
.

Assume the regression model (
98
) and the conditions for
\epsilon
,
Z
,
f
in
6.11
. If
J_{r}<J_{s}
and
J_{r}/J_{s}
either converges to
0
or stay bounded away from
0
as
n
increases, then there is a positive constant
\kappa
depending only on the model such that

(a)

\sqrt{n}\frac{\nabla_{i}\ell^{(r,s)}(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})}{\sigma_{n}^{(r,s)}}\text{ is }\left[\left(\sqrt{\frac{J_{s}J_{r}^{a}}{n}}+\frac{J_{s}J_{r}^{a/2}}{n}\right)\kappa\,,~2\right]\text{-SW}\,;

(100)

(b)

\frac{n\nabla_{i}\left[R^{(r)}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})-R^{(s)}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\right]}{\sigma_{n}^{(r,s)}}\text{ is }\left(\sqrt{\frac{J_{s}J_{r}^{a}}{n}}\kappa\,,~2\right)\text{-SW}\,.

(101)

We first note that the optimal choice of
J
with minimum risk is
J^{*}\asymp n^{1/(1+a)}
. Thus, in order for the scaling parameter in the RHS of (
100
) and (
101
) to be
o(1)
, one needs
J_{s}J_{r}^{a}\ll n=(J^{*})^{1+a}
. This is possible if both
J_{s}
,
J_{r}
are no larger than
J^{*}
, or
J_{s}
is slightly larger than
J^{*}
but
J_{r}
is sufficiently small. In other words, the stability condition is more likely to hold when comparing over-smoothing estimators. This condition is violated if
J_{r}\geq J^{*}
, suggesting that the stability condition required by the CLT may not hold for under-smoothing estimators. Such subtleties in the stability condition of the difference loss resembles the well-known behavior of the regular CV: it can eliminate inferior estimators with large bias (over-smoothing) but struggles to distinguish slightly overfitting estimators.

When
J_{r}/J_{s}
stays bounded away from
0
, then the sufficient stability condition required by CLT becomes
J_{s}\ll J^{*}
, which means that if the two estimators under comparison are too similar, the stability conditions can only hold for over-smoothing estimators.

Proof of
Proposition

6.12
.

Denote

\delta_{j}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}Z_{i}-f_{j}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\epsilon_{i}Z_{i}+f(Z_{i})Z_{i}-f_{j}\right]\,,

which satisfies

\mathbb{E}\delta_{j}=0
and
{\rm Var}(\delta_{j})=\sigma_{j}^{2}/n
for some
\sigma_{j}^{2}\in[\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2},\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+c]
for a constant
c
depending only on
f
.

For positive integers
u<v
, define

\|f_{u,v}\|^{2}=\sum_{j=u+1}^{v}f_{j}^{2}\,.

The condition
f_{j}\asymp j^{-(1+a)/2}
implies that

\sqrt{u-v}\cdot v^{-(1+a)/2}\lesssim\|f_{u,v}\|\lesssim\sqrt{u-v}\cdot u^{-(1+a)/2}\,.

We use a shorthand notation for the difference of two loss functions
\ell^{(r)}(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
and
\ell^{(s)}(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
:

\displaystyle T\coloneqq

\displaystyle(Y_{0}-\hat{f}^{(r)}(Z_{0}))^{2}-(Y_{0}-\hat{f}^{(s)}(Z_{0}))^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\epsilon_{0}(\hat{f}^{(s)}(Z_{0})-\hat{f}^{(r)}(Z_{0}))+\left[2f(Z_{0})-\hat{f}^{(r)}(Z_{0})-\hat{f}^{(s)}(Z_{0})\right]\left[\hat{f}^{(s)}(Z_{0})-\hat{f}^{(r)}(Z_{0})\right]\,.

We first provide a lower bound of
\sigma_{n}^{(r,s)}
, which, by definition, equals

\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}[\mathbb{E}(T|X_{0})]}
. Recall that
{\mathcal{D}}_{n}=(X_{i}:1\leq i\leq n)
.
A straightforward algebra leads to

\mathbb{E}(T|X_{0})=2\epsilon_{0}\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}f_{j}Z_{0j}+h(Z_{0})

where
h(Z_{0})
is a deterministic function of
Z_{0}
. As a result

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\left[\mathbb{E}(T|X_{0})\right]\geq

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left[2\epsilon_{0}\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}f_{j}Z_{0j}\right]=4\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}f_{j}^{2}\,,

implying that

\sigma_{n}^{(r,s)}\geq 2\sigma_{\epsilon}\|f_{J_{r},J_{s}}\|\,.

(102)

Now take the
\nabla_{i}
operator. To derive a simple expression of
\nabla_{i}T
, we first expand
T
:

\displaystyle T=

\displaystyle 2\epsilon_{0}\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}\hat{f}_{j}Z_{0j}

\displaystyle+\left[-2\sum_{j=1}^{J_{r}}\delta_{j}Z_{0j}+\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}(f_{j}-\delta_{j})Z_{0j}+2\sum_{j=J_{s}+1}^{\infty}f_{j}Z_{0j}\right]\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}\hat{f}_{j}Z_{0j}\,,

so that

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}T=

\displaystyle 2\epsilon_{0}\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}(\nabla_{i}\delta_{j})Z_{0j}

\displaystyle+\left[-2\sum_{j=1}^{J_{r}}(\nabla_{i}\delta_{j})Z_{0j}-\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}(\nabla_{i}\delta_{j})Z_{0j}\right]\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}\hat{f}_{j}Z_{0j}

\displaystyle+\left[-2\sum_{j=1}^{J_{r}}\delta_{j}Z_{0j}+\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}(f_{j}-\delta_{j})Z_{0j}+2\sum_{j=J_{s}+1}^{\infty}f_{j}Z_{0j}\right]\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}(\nabla_{i}\delta_{j})Z_{0j}

\displaystyle\coloneqq

\displaystyle A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3}\,,

where
\nabla_{i}\delta_{j}=n^{-1}(Y_{i}^{\prime}Z^{\prime}_{ij}-Y_{i}Z_{ij})
.

Through a simple exercise using
Theorem

2.14
and
Theorem

2.13
, one can show that
Y_{i}
is
(\kappa,1/2)
-SW, and
\delta_{j}
is
(\kappa/\sqrt{n},1)
-SW for a constant
\kappa
depending on the model only.

Then
Proposition

2.11
and
Theorem

2.14
further imply the following (with a potentially different value of constant
\kappa
:

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle\text{ is }\left(\frac{\sqrt{J_{s}-J_{r}}}{n}\kappa,~1\right)\text{-SW}\,,

(103)

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle\text{ is }\left[\frac{\sqrt{J_{s}}}{n}\left(\|f_{J_{r},J_{s}}\|+\frac{\sqrt{J_{s}-J_{r}}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\kappa\,,~2\right]\text{-SW}\,,

(104)

\displaystyle A_{3}

\displaystyle\text{ is }\left[\left(\frac{\sqrt{J_{s}}}{\sqrt{n}}+\|f_{J_{r},\infty}\|\right)\frac{\sqrt{J_{s}-J_{r}}}{n}\kappa\,,~2\right]\text{-SW}\,.

(105)

In order to get concrete bounds for
\|f_{J_{r},J_{s}}\|
, we first consider the scenario
J_{r}/J_{s}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

In the case
J_{r}/J_{s}\rightarrow 0
we have

\displaystyle\|f_{J_{r},J_{s}}\|\asymp

\displaystyle J_{r}^{-a/2}\,.

Plug this into (
102
), (
103
), (
104
), (
105
) we obtain
(
100
).

Now we work on the risk stability. Under our model, for any truncation value
J
we have

R({\mathcal{D}}_{n})=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\delta_{j}^{2}+\sum_{j=J+1}^{\infty}f_{j}^{2}\,,

and

R^{(r)}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})-R^{(s)}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})=-\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}\delta_{j}^{2}+\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}f_{j}^{2}\,,

hence

\nabla_{i}\left[R^{(r)}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})-R^{(s)}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\right]=-2\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J_{s}}\nabla_{i}(\delta_{j}^{2})\,.

By definition

\nabla_{i}(\delta_{j}^{2})=\delta_{j}(\nabla_{i}\delta_{j})+\delta_{j}^{i}(\nabla_{i}\delta_{j})

which is
(n^{-3/2}\kappa,~3/2)
-SW. Now apply
Theorem

2.16
to the sequence
(\sum_{j=J_{r}+1}^{J}\nabla_{i}(\delta_{j}^{2}):J_{r}<J\leq J_{s})
with respect to the
\sigma
-field filtration
\{{\mathcal{D}}_{n}\cup\{X_{i1},...,X_{i,J}\}:J_{r}<J\leq J_{s}\}
we conclude that

\nabla_{i}\left[R^{(r)}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})-R^{(s)}({\mathcal{D}}_{n})\right]\text{ is }\left(n^{-3/2}J_{s}^{1/2}\kappa\,,~2\right)\text{-SW}

and hence (
101
) follows.

In the other case,
J_{r}\geq cJ_{s}
for a constant
c>0
. Now

\|f_{J_{r},J_{s}}\|\asymp J_{s}^{-(1+a)/2}\sqrt{J_{s}-J_{r}}
and the remaining proof is the same as in the case of
J_{r}/J_{s}\rightarrow 0
.
∎

6.4
Stability of Online Algorithms

In this subsection, we provide examples of the stability of online algorithms, specifically addressing (
27
) in
3.11
.
We focus on a simplified setting where the basis functions are given by the individual coordinates of the covariate vector
Z
, that is,
\phi_{j}(Z_{i})=Z_{ij}
. Under this simplification, the basis expansion estimator in (
24
) reduces to the setting considered in
Section

6.3
.

Notation Declaration:
In this subsection we will explicitly track the evolution of each coordinate of
\hat{f}
over time. Thus we need to use the following notation.

•

f=(f_{j}:j\geq 1)
denotes the true vector of coefficients.

•

\hat{f}_{i}=(\hat{f}_{i,j}:j\geq 1)
denotes the estimated vector of coefficients at the end of step
i
, whose
j
th coordinate is
\hat{f}_{i,j}
. Note that this notation
\hat{f}_{i}
has a different meaning than the notation “
\hat{f}_{j}
” in
Section

6.3
(there
\hat{f}_{j}
means the
j
th coordinate of the estimated coefficient
\hat{f}
.) Generally speaking, in this subsection, the symbol “
i
” (and similarly “
t
” and “
n
”) is used to denote a time point or sample size, whereas the symbol “
j
” is used to denote a coordinate of
Z
or
f
.

•

\hat{f}_{i}^{\prime}
and
\hat{f}_{i,j}^{\prime}
correspond to the perturb-one version of
\hat{f}_{i}
and
\hat{f}_{i,j}
respectively, with the perturbed sample point obvious from the context.

•

(J_{n}:n\geq 1)
is a sequence of positive numbers indicating the number of basis functions used by sieve SGD at sample size
n
.

Batch truncated series estimate

To warm up,
first consider the truncated estimator
\hat{f}^{(J_{n})}
given in (
99
). The derivation from the previous subsection implies that

\mathbb{E}\left\{[\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{t}(X_{t+1})]^{2}|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\right\}\lesssim J_{t}t^{-2}
. So that if
J_{t}\asymp t^{\tau}
then (
27
) holds with
b=1-\tau/2
. If the true coefficient
f
belongs to a Sobolev ellipsoid:

W(\varpi,L)\coloneqq\left\{f:~\sum_{j\geq 1}j^{2\varpi}f_{j}^{2}\leq L\right\}

(106)

for a pair of positive constants
(\varpi,L)
, then the minimax optimal error rate is achieved by
J_{n}\asymp n^{1/(1+2\varpi)}
. Now consider two estimators with basis cardinality sequences
J_{n,1}=n^{\tau_{1}}
and
J_{n,2}\asymp n^{\tau_{2}}
respectively, with
J_{n,1}
being the better choice. The sufficient stability condition for wRV to consistently select the better sequence
J_{n,1}
is quite similar to that in
Section

6.3
:
\tau_{2}
needs to be either (i) smaller than
\tau^{*}=1/(1+2\varpi)
while
\tau_{1}
being equal to or slightly larger than
\tau^{*}
,
or (ii) slightly larger than
\tau^{*}
while
\tau_{1}
sufficiently small. In other words, wRV, like CV, is theoretically more reliable in eliminating highly biased estimators (small
\tau_{2}
versus moderate
\tau_{1}
), or comparing two very different estimators (moderately large
\tau_{2}
and small
\tau_{1}
).

Sieve SGD

To illustrate a more challenging and less explored regime of online estimator stability, we
consider the sieve SGD estimator defined in (
24
). Due to its relatively recent introduction, theoretical analyses of convergence rates of general sieve SGD algorithms using non-optimal tuning values are not yet available. Thus the stability analysis of sieve SGD is still an ongoing line of research. Here we provide some initial results in this direction.

In the
simplified setting with data generated from model (
98
) and the corresponding basis
\phi_{j}(Z_{i})=Z_{ij}
, the original form of sieve SGD update (
24
) reduces to

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{0}=

\displaystyle 0\,,

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{i}=

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{i-1}+\alpha_{i}(Y_{i}-\langle\hat{f}_{i-1},~Z_{i}\rangle)\Lambda_{i}Z_{i}\,,\quad i\geq 1\,,

(107)

where
\alpha_{i}
is the learning rate,
\Lambda_{i}
is a diagonal truncation/shrinkage matrix whose
j
th diagonal entry equals
j^{-2w}\mathds{1}(j\leq J_{i})
, and
J_{i}
is the number of basis functions used at sample size
i
. As discussed in
Section

3.2
, the tuning parameter of sieve SGD is the entire sequence
(J_{i}:i\geq 1)
, which typically takes the form
J_{i}\asymp i^{\tau}
. The multiplier
j^{-2w}
provides coordinate-wise shrinkage, which is introduced in the original sieve SGD algorithm.

Proposition 6.13
.

Assume i.i.d. data from (
98
) satisfying
6.11
. Let
\{\hat{f}_{i}:i\geq 1\}
be the sieve SGD estimate (
107
) with basis size
J_{i}=i^{-\tau}
, learning rate
\alpha_{i}=ci^{-a}
, and shrinkage exponent
w>1/2
, such that
a+2w\tau<1
and
c<\sum_{j\geq 1}j^{-2w}
. Suppose
|Y_{i}-\langle\hat{f}_{i-1},~Z_{i}\rangle|
is uniformly bounded by a constant almost surely for all
i\geq 1
then we have, for any
t\geq i\geq 1

\mathbb{E}\left[\|\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{t}(Z_{t+1})\|^{2}\bigg|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\right]\lesssim t^{-2a}\,,~~\text{almost surely}\,.

Proposition

6.13
is an example of stability without strong convexity. It should be compared to
Theorem

6.5
, which shows a similar rate under strong convexity of the objective function. The argument relies on taking the conditional expectation of the SGD objective function given the previous estimate, and exploit the strong convexity of this conditional expectation.

Remark 6.14
.

Proposition

6.13
involves a number of technical conditions, most of which are for simplicity of presentation. The most unnatural assumption is the uniform boundedness of the residual
Y_{i}-\langle\hat{f}_{i-1},~Z_{i}\rangle
. This can be achieved if we further restrict the estimates
\hat{f}_{i}
in a bounded set such as a Sobolev ellipsoid, which can be realized by a further projection step at the end of each iteration. Alternatively, one may obtain weaker versions of
Proposition

6.13
, such as moment or sub-Weibull bound instead of
L_{\infty}
norm bound.

Remark 6.15
.

In order to illustrate the relevance of
Proposition

6.13
in the context of
Theorem

3.12
, we need to characterize the risk convergence rate of sieve SGD estimates obtained from general combinations of tuning parameters. Such results are not yet directly available in the current literature due to the recent appearance of sieve SGD. However, there is evidence that sieve SGD may have the same convergence rate as the batch mode truncated basis expansion estimate using
J_{n}
basis functions at sample size
n
. Proceeding with this hypothesis and assuming the true coefficient
f
belongs to the Sobolev ellipsoid
W(\varpi,L)
in (
106
), consider two sieve SGD estimators with
J_{i}=i^{\tau_{r}}
,
\alpha_{i}=ci^{-a}
for
r=1,2
. Suppose
\tau_{1}=\tau^{*}=1/(1+2\varpi)
is a better choice than
\tau_{2}
. Using the batch mode error rate and stability bound in
Proposition

6.13
, the stability requirements in
Theorem

3.12
reduces to

1.

a>1/2+\varpi\tau_{2}
when
\tau_{2}<\tau_{1}
(under-fitting);

2.

a>1-\tau_{2}/2
when
\tau_{2}>\tau_{1}
(over-fitting).

A caveat here is that such choices of
(a,\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
are incompatible with the requirement of
a+2w\tau<1
in
Proposition

6.13
unless we allow for
w<1/4
. We further conjecture that this requirement is an artifact of the current proof and can be improved using a more refined argument. Intuitively, a larger value of
w
implies stronger shrinkage, hence making the estimate more stable. An evidence supporting this conjecture is that one can establish consistency of sieve SGD with
w=0
. See
Section

6.5
for relevant literature reviews.

Proof of
Proposition

6.13
.

Let
\eta_{t}=\nabla_{i}\hat{f}_{t}
.
For
t>i
, we have, by construction

\eta_{t}=\eta_{t-1}-\alpha_{t}\Lambda_{t}Z_{t}Z_{t}^{T}\eta_{t-1}\,.

(108)

Let
\tilde{\eta}_{t}=\Lambda_{t}^{-1/2}\eta_{t}
and
\tilde{Z}_{t}=\Lambda_{t}^{1/2}Z_{t}
(with
0/0=0
), then (
108
) becomes

\tilde{\eta}_{t}=\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}-\alpha_{t}\tilde{Z}_{t}\tilde{Z}_{t}^{T}\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}\,.

(109)

We first control
\|\tilde{\eta}_{i}\|
in a similar fashion as in
Lemma

6.6
:

\|\tilde{\eta}_{i}\|=\left\|\alpha_{i}(Y_{i}-\langle\hat{f}_{i-1},~Z_{i}\rangle)\tilde{Z}_{i}-\alpha_{i}(Y_{i}^{\prime}-\langle\hat{f}_{i-1},~Z_{i}^{\prime}\rangle)\tilde{Z}_{i}^{\prime}\right\|\lesssim\alpha_{i}\,,

(110)

because, by assumption,
\|\tilde{Z}_{i}\|\leq C
for
w>1/2
and
Y_{i}-\langle\hat{f}_{i-1},~Z_{i}\rangle
is uniformly bounded.

Taking squared norm on both sides of (
109
), we have

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\eta}_{t}\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}\|^{2}-(2\alpha_{t}-\alpha_{t}^{2}\|\tilde{Z}_{t}\|^{2})\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}^{T}\tilde{Z}_{t}\tilde{Z}_{t}^{T}\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}^{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}\|^{2}-\alpha_{t}\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}^{T}\tilde{Z}_{t}\tilde{Z}_{t}^{T}\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}^{T}\,,

because
\alpha_{t}\|\tilde{Z}_{t}\|^{2}\leq 1
by the choice of constant
c
.

Dividing both sides by
\|\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}\|^{2}
and take conditional expectation given
{\mathcal{D}}_{t-1}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\|\tilde{\eta}_{t}\|^{2}}{\|\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}\|^{2}}\bigg|{\mathcal{D}}_{t-1}\right]\leq 1-\alpha_{t}\frac{\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}^{T}\Lambda_{t}\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}}{\|\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}\|^{2}}\leq 1-\alpha_{t}J_{t}^{-2w}\leq e^{-\alpha_{t}J_{t}^{-2w}}\,,

(111)

where the second inequality follows from that the minimum effective eigenvalue (those multiplied to the non-zero entries of
\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}
) is at least
J_{t}^{-2w}
.

Now using the telescoping version of (
111
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\tilde{\eta}_{t}\|^{2}\big|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\right]\leq

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\eta}_{i}\|^{2}\exp\left(-\sum_{l=i+1}^{t}\alpha_{l}J_{l}^{-2w}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\eta}_{i}\|^{2}\exp\left(-c\sum_{l=i+1}^{t}l^{-a-2w\tau}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle i^{-2a}\exp\left[-c^{\prime}\left((t+1)^{1-a-2w\tau}-(i+1)^{1-a-2w\tau}\right)\right]\,.

The claim follows from the same argument as in
Lemma

6.6
by considering
i\leq t/2
and
i\geq t/2
.
∎

Inspecting the proof of
Proposition

6.13
. The main source of potential (and likely) looseness that is responsible for the counterintuitive dependence on
w
seems to be bounding

\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}^{T}\Lambda_{t}\tilde{\eta}_{i-1}\geq J_{t}^{-2w}\|\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}\|_{2}^{2}

in (
111
). The diagonal entries of
\Lambda_{t}
have very difference magnitudes. On the other hand, the shrinkage mechanism in the sieve SGD update suggests that
|\hat{f}_{t,j}|
decays at a speed of
j^{-2w}
as
j
increases. Thus
\tilde{\eta}_{t-1,j}=j^{w}(\hat{f}_{t-1,j}-\hat{f}_{t-1,j}^{\prime})
should carry a scaling of
j^{-w}
. So intuitively, in the product
\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}^{T}\Lambda_{t}\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}
, large entries in
\Lambda_{t}
are aligned with large entries of
\tilde{\eta}_{t-1}
and it is overly conservative to use a lower bound based on the smallest positive eigenvalue of
\Lambda_{t}
.

6.5
Bibliographic Notes

Stability of regularized M-estimators that are suitable for conditions in
Section

2
and
Section

4
have been established in
Bousquet and Elisseeff 2002
and
Austern and Zhou 2024
, respectively. These results are concerned with the global optimum of the M-estimation problem, without taking into account the optimization error. First order stability of SGD under strong convexity and smooth gradients has been established in
Hardt et al. 2016
, which is covered as a special case of our
Theorem

6.5
. Our
Proposition

6.4
is Lemma 3.7.3 of
Hardt et al. 2016
, who gave an elegant proof. The second-order stability of SGD presented in
Section

6.2
is first developed in
Kissel and Lei 2022
.

The stability of (the standardized) difference loss function considered in
Section

6.3
is adapted from
Kissel and Lei 2022
, which contains numerical examples of the first and second order stability of the difference loss function. The stability of the difference risk function in Part (b) of
Proposition

6.12
is new and will lead to deterministic centering CLT for the CV estimate of expected difference risks.

As an example illustrating the necessity of Part 3 of
6.11
,

Zhang et al. 2023
considered a similar nonparametric regression setting, where the coefficients
f_{j}
alternate between zero and
j^{-2}
according to
\lfloor\log_{3}[\log_{2}(j)]\rfloor
being even or odd. In that paper, the purpose is to understand the ability of cross-validation to pick the correct model between two models,
J_{r}
and
J_{s}
, such that
J_{r}=J_{s}-1
. If we use this sequence of
(f_{j}:j\geq 1)
in the setting of
Proposition

6.12
and assume
J_{s}\ll n^{1/5}
, then the proof can be adapted to show that
\nabla_{1}\ell^{(r,s)}(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})/\sigma_{n}^{(r,s)}=o_{P}(1)
if and only if
f_{J_{s}}\neq 0
.
Such a dichotomy originates from the variance of the loss difference
\ell^{(r,s)}(X_{0},{\mathcal{D}}_{n})
. When
f_{J_{s}}=0
, the difference between the two true models is zero and any variance in the loss difference will be from the random fluctuation in the estimated coefficient
\hat{f}_{J_{s}}
. On the other hand, when
f_{J_{s}}\neq 0
, there is a bias term contributing to the loss difference. We do not expect Gaussian approximation to hold for the former case due to the severe degeneracy of the loss difference.

The original idea of sieve SGD first appeared in
Dieuleveut and Bach 2016
.
The form of sieve SGD considered in this article is proposed in
Zhang and Simon 2022
, which established its near optimality in terms of error rates and storage cost (see also
Quan and Lin 2024
).
A version of sieve SGD implemented without shrinkage (
w=0
) is shown to achieve optimal convergence rate in
Chen and Klusowski 2024
, providing evidence of potential improved stability analysis with better dependence on the shrinkage parameter.
The stability property in
Proposition

6.13
is adapted from
Zhang and Lei 2023
.

Another important general method for designing stable algorithms is re-sampling. In
Bühlmann and Yu 2002
, it has been shown that the bootstrap-aggregating (bagging) method can turn unstable algorithms such as decision trees into stable ones. Similar bagging ideas are also used in stability-based variable selection
(
Meinshausen and Bühlmann 2010
;
Liu et al. 2010
)
. A more systematic study of bagging and re-sampling methods for algorithmic stability in the context of predictive inference has been initiated in
Soloff et al. 2024a
;
Soloff et al. 2024b
.
